Graphical Abstract. Privacy boundaries across persistent updates, release, and withdrawal. 

Personal agents carry private experience into later decisions through persistent memory. This thematic survey compares memory admission, maintenance, release, and withdrawal by protected change, observer, and validation setting. Direct evidence in the screened corpus comes from controlled memory workflows. Three worked scenarios follow one private fact through booking, feedback-driven learning, and withdrawal. We show when prompt revision and memory reads compose, when skills inherit a private head’s bound, and why reader non-use does not establish cleanup of learned state. Diagnostic experiments measure the utility costs of wider label protection and broad memory deletion, and test how missing lineage obscures descendants. CCS Concepts: • Security and privacy → Privacy protections ; • Computing methodologies → Intelli-gent agents ; Continual learning ; • Information systems → Personalization .Additional Key Words and Phrases: personal agents, self-evolution, privacy protection, continual learning, long-term memory, machine unlearning 

1 Introduction 

A scheduling assistant may retain a user’s medical restriction in long-term memory and later use it to propose an appointment. That use has several privacy boundaries. A cloud host can observe a memory access, a booking service can observe the chosen time, and a future session can recover the restriction from a derived summary. If the same restriction also informs a prompt revision or a reusable skill, the pathways multiply. We survey the protection of persistent personal memory and examine what changes when the same private fact reaches another carrier. Recent benchmarks localize concrete leakage along individual agent interfaces. PrivacyLens [96] exposes gaps between normative privacy awareness and actual disclosure during multi-step execu-tion. On long-term memory, MEXTRA [114] extracts private interactions via black-box prompting, 

Authors’ Contact Information: Zibin Lin, Shenzhen University, Shenzhen, China, linaacc9595@gmail.com; Taotao Wang, Shenzhen University, Shenzhen, China, ttwang@szu.edu.cn; Shengli Zhang, Shenzhen University, Shenzhen, China, zsl@ szu.edu.cn. arXiv:2611.26983v1 [cs.CR] 20 Nov 2026 2 Zibin Lin, Taotao Wang, and Shengli Zhang 

and SPORE [38] leaks stored secrets into external tool-call parameters across sessions. At the skill layer, AntiSkillBench [128] measures content leakage, attribute disclosure, and behavioral imper-sonation induced by persona skills distilled from personal trajectories. Together, these benchmarks expose interface-level vulnerabilities while leaving open how privacy risk propagates from raw inputs across persistent carriers to external outputs. We define a self-evolving personal agent as an agent that serves one user over time and updates parameters, prompt policies, memories, or skills from experiences, preferences, corrections, or tool feedback [37]. PAHF illustrates the feedback loop: pre-action clarification and post-action correction update a per-user memory that can affect the next action [69]. We center the survey on memory write, maintenance, read, release, and withdrawal. We include results on fixed personal profiles when they evaluate a release surface and study prompt, parameter, preference, and skill methods where they clarify a specific transfer from or interaction with memory. We also separate two complementary privacy objectives. Privacy-aware evolution studies how an agent learns contextual disclosure norms, deciding which attributes a task may consult or share with a recipient. Privacy-protected evolution constrains the update process itself, bounding what untrusted endpoints or external observers infer from candidate scores, update logs, or modified state carriers. Personal assistants such as PANOLA [113] and uncertainty-aware decision agents [3] learn disclosure preferences and negotiate release, while their reported release evaluations do not establish privacy guarantees for learning transcripts or persistent state updates. The graphical abstract summarizes our synthesis, and Figure 1 maps operator interventions across the loop. Table 1 situates this review among surveys of adaptation, state governance, and privacy. SAVER tracks where experience acquires influence and which observations reveal a failure [10]. Always-On Agents links durable state to provenance, recovery, and externally committed effects [27]. The RAG accounting survey distinguishes protected units, accounting horizons, and release boundaries [149]. We connect these perspectives by tracking a change to the same personal fact through each operation and asking which guarantees survive when an observer sees several outputs. We derive a joint bound for bounded prompt revision and memory reads, and locate its failure at an action that consults raw memory. Three questions guide the comparison: 

• RQ1 (Protection units). Which changes to persistent personal memory do mechanisms protect, and how do record, attribute, tuple, or user-level units map to the same fact across carriers? 

• RQ2 (Observers and endpoints). Which observations can a host, validator, or recipient link, and under what conditions do protections cover that joint view? 

• RQ3 (Persistence and verification). What evidence supports privacy after a fresh write, artifact revision, or withdrawal, and which conditions govern transfer beyond memory? We map memory protection by operation and follow one fact through booking, feedback-driven learning, and withdrawal. For each scenario we identify which results apply to the specified history change and observer, which fall outside scope, and which lack evidence at the target interface. Sections 2–3 define the scope and coding. Sections 4–6 review the mechanisms, Section 7 compares their boundaries and costs, and Section 8 identifies research priorities. 

2 Review Scope and Methodology 2.1 Research Design and Literature Scope 

This thematic survey covers work publicly available through 10 November 2026. Its core is privacy protection for persistent memory in agents that serve one user over time. Prompt, parameter, preference, and skill studies enter when they identify a mechanism or comparator that constrains Privacy Protection in Self-Evolving Personal Agents 3

The initial search combined thematic queries, archival checks, and one-hop citation tracking. The carrier audit used five fielded arXiv queries and five title and abstract filters over 24,844 ACL Anthology proceedings records. The channels returned 216 and 102 hits, respectively, yielding 191 and 98 unique records within their respective channels. We matched stable identifiers and titles, screened all hits, and checked primary texts for new inclusions. The audit recovered five of nine known direct studies; all nine concern memory, so this check does not calibrate coverage of other carriers. Follow-ups targeted linked observation and withdrawal, and rescreened 40 operation-overlap decisions. Supplementary Sections S1.14–S1.16 give the windows and dispositions. 

2.2 Inclusion, Exclusion, and Version Selection 

Mechanism eligibility requires an accessible primary source by the cutoff, an explicit privacy intervention at an identifiable operation, and enough detail to verify at least two of the protected 4 Zibin Lin, Taotao Wang, and Shengli Zhang 

unit, observer, and trusted endpoints. The operation must act on persistent adaptation, constrain the use or release of retained state, or provide a transferable primitive for one of those steps. Deployment feasibility alone, without a privacy outcome or an applicable analysis, does not count as evidence for protection. We exclude from mechanism coding static personalization or one-shot inference lacking per-sistent state or protected read interfaces, “on-device” or “federated” claims lacking a localizable operator, purely offensive extraction studies, and untraceable summaries. Related surveys and benchmarks supply definitions, threat surfaces, or baselines. A benchmark contributes to mecha-nism counts when it specifies and supports a protection step with a localized operator, a protected object, and a defined observer or trust boundary. We retain independent studies of the same op-eration when they contribute a matched comparison, conflicting result, or measured trade-off. Duplicate preprints, OpenReview revisions, and archival publications from one method family collapse to one entry, preferring peer-reviewed archival versions published by 10 November 2026 or a version-pinned preprint available by the cutoff; post-cutoff releases lie outside our scope. 

2.3 Evidence Coding and Synthesis Protocol 

Supplementary Section S5 records one row per source, protection operation, and evidentiary setting when the source supports distinct claims. Rows identify the carrier, protected change or object, observable interface, and supporting result or specification. Validation settings distinguish formal results, controlled or simulated evaluations, and deployment evidence. The 22 examples in Supplementary Table S11 also record trusted endpoints, reuse conditions, and uncovered exposures in detail. Evidence distance describes the setting of each operation: 

• D (Direct update intervention): Evidence covers protection of persistent state written, maintained, or withdrawn for one user and carried across decisions or interactions. Con-trolled loops qualify. Optimizer iterations and held-out tests on a selected candidate do not by themselves establish carryover from a committed update. 

• S (Surface or release intervention): Evaluates reading, minimizing, or releasing retained user state in a personal-agent task. 

• T (Transferable mechanism or primitive): Supports a relevant operation in another setting, including general algorithms and shared federated updates. Guarantee type and evidence distance answer separate questions. An empirical write gate may receive D while a formal composition theorem receives T. A counted operation needs a reported evaluation or an analytical result for the stated protection claim. We record shared pipeline outcomes at the implicated operations and leave the isolated effect of each component unassigned. Design descriptions and worked examples without such support provide background. Supplementary Table S10 counts each source once at its closest supported operation ( D, then S, then T). Section S5 reports the distribution by operation and distinct sources within each family. Supplementary Section S3.3 documents an independent recoding of selected boundary cases and shows which conclusions depend on those rulings. Table 2 assigns one primary role per source. Risk and evaluation studies can also contribute a tested intervention, as recorded in Supplementary Table S10. Supplementary Table S9 gives all source assignments and Section S5 gives the operation ledger. One contextual graph-model reference lies outside the corpus [133]. 

3 From Technical Labels to an Update-Operator Framework 

Differential privacy can protect prompt scores, the training of a reward head, adapter updates, or multi-round memory reads. Each use has its own protected change, observer, trusted endpoints, and Privacy Protection in Self-Evolving Personal Agents 5

composition rule. We trace protection operations along the chain from private inputs through eval-uation and updates to persistent state, downstream release, and withdrawal. The four dimensions below connect each guarantee to the part of that chain it covers. 

Dimension 1: State Carriers and Intervention Operators. The first dimension pairs each persistent carrier with the exact update or usage operator constrained by a mechanism. Parameter protections act on gradients, adapters, or reward signals; prompt protections modify generation, scoring, or selection; preference protections bound comparison tuples, choice labels, or policy sampling; and memory or skill protections govern writeback, maintenance, withdrawal, release, or repository admission. Localizing the operator determines which flows are constrained. Keeping conversations on-device reduces upload exposure, yet offers no persistent update bound if distilled summaries enter shared skill repositories or if cloud servers observe accepted prompt revisions over time. 

Dimension 2: Protection Units and Intra-User Adjacency. The second dimension fixes the protection unit by specifying which inputs may differ. Participation privacy changes one person’s contribution to a cohort. Intra-user privacy can change a fact, episode, attribute, or time window in the same person’s history. When a fact changes 𝑘 records, the generic group conversion sends 𝜀 to 𝑘𝜀 and 𝛿 

to 𝛿 Í𝑘 −1 

> 𝑗 =0

𝑒 𝑗𝜀 . A mechanism calibrated to the group can establish a different bound. Pufferfish [59] compares outputs conditioned on alternative secrets under a specified family of history distributions. It can represent a fact whose evidence is dispersed across turns, with the correlation model and observable outputs made explicit. 

Dimension 3: Observable Interfaces and Trusted Endpoints. The third dimension identifies the adversary’s observable interface (or transcript ) alongside required trusted endpoints . Releasing a DP prompt or reward head does not conceal raw training records seen by a trusted curator. Secure multi-party computation (MPC) hides secret-shared inputs from non-colluding servers during execution, yet permitted outputs still reflect those inputs. Entity masking relies on detector recall and local mapping isolation, while trusted execution environments (TEEs) depend on hardware integrity, remote attestation, and declared access-pattern leakage functions. Each mechanism therefore answers a distinct observer question and demands explicit trust boundaries. 

Dimension 4: Temporal Horizon, Replay, and Derived-State Withdrawal. The fourth dimension tracks continual reuse and derived-artifact propagation. Computation on privatized outputs and public inputs inherits the original DP bound by post-processing. Fresh access to private state requires checking the resulting observation and any applicable composition rule. Likewise, deleting 6 Zibin Lin, Taotao Wang, and Shengli Zhang Private experience

4 Privacy Mechanisms in Parameter, Prompt, and Preference Updates 4.1 Candidate Generation, Scoring, and Selection in Prompt Optimization 

Prompt self-evolution creates persistent exposure whenever private interaction records shape candidate instructions, exemplars, or rankings. Reviewed mechanisms place formal protection at candidate generation, scoring, or selection. DP-OPT [50] runs a local differentially private (DP) ensemble using LimitedDomain approximate-DP token generation (accumulating per-token failure 𝛿 0 across 𝑚 generated tokens) over private training records, then applies the exponential mechanism on a private validation split so an untrusted cloud model receives only a DP prompt. Parallel composition across generation and selection requires disjointness at the target intra-user protection unit rather than merely by record ID. By contrast, DP-ES [76] withholds private data from the mutation model and focuses its privacy budget on Gaussian-noised evaluation scores for 𝐾 

candidate prompts per round. Each candidate is scored on a random minibatch (without-replacement subsampling 𝑞 = 𝑏 /𝑛 under replace-one adjacency), after which top-𝑘 selection, mutation, and release follow by post-processing. The two methods also induce distinct trust boundaries: DP-OPT trusts a local generator and validator, while DP-ES trusts a scoring endpoint with private evaluation records. Its Rényi differential privacy (RDP) accountant tracks privacy loss across scoring calls. Privacy Protection in Self-Evolving Personal Agents 7

Prompt carriers span discrete text, continuous soft prompts, and reusable demonstrations. Duan et al. [30] introduce two standalone mechanisms. PromptDPSGD trains continuous soft prompts via white-box DP-SGD (per-example clipping and Gaussian noise) and deploys the continuous prompt directly; PromptPATE partitions private data across disjoint teacher prompts to label an unlabeled public dataset via PATE noisy class-label voting, producing discrete demonstrations. Unlike DP-OPT, PromptPATE requires unlabeled public inputs. Differentially private few-shot generation [109] similarly synthesizes reusable DP exemplars from fixed datasets in a single pass. Migrating these primitives to continual user feedback requires tracking release multiplicity, sample reuse, and intra-user correlation across retraining rounds. Structural assumptions dictate which conditions require re-verification in a personal agent. A single user’s history typically feeds both prompt revision and behavioral evaluation; if an intra-user fact recurs across DP-OPT’s training and validation splits, parallel composition fails for that fact and reverts to sequential composition with group scaling. Similarly, DP-ES forfeits minibatch amplification ( 𝑞 = 𝑏 /𝑛 ) if candidates are scored deterministically over a small recent-turn buffer, and 𝑇 -round iterative feedback composes up to 𝑇 𝐾 RDP evaluations. DP-ES reports a matched task comparison with DP-OPT under a common conservative privacy upper bound [76]. Its accuracy advantage is larger on GSM8K and MedQA, with close results on BANKING77 and Alpaca. The reported task results and resource costs depend on the benchmark settings and the bound is not an exact budget match. When one user contributes correlated interactions, whole-history protection requires an explicit contribution cap and re-accounting, while intra-user fact protection requires defining an intra-user adjacency unit. Private selection from private candidates [74] targets meta-selection over already-DP candidate oracles when quality scores lack low sensitivity. Randomized stopping releases a winning candidate and conceals rejected draws, bounding privacy loss by a constant factor (2 𝜀 1 or 3 𝜀 1) for pure (𝜀 1, 0)-DP base oracles; approximate (𝜀 1, 𝛿 1)-DP oracles ( 𝛿 1 > 0) incur a stopping-horizon 𝛿 blow-up (∝ 𝛿 1/𝛾 ) plus additive 𝜀 overhead. Coded T, this single-pass operator still requires extension to repeated sessions and continual writeback. Effective prompt privacy therefore begins by locating where private inputs enter—generation, scoring, or selection—before fixing the adjacency unit and budget schedule. Hanke et al. compare locally generated DP prompts or demonstrations with local private weight updates on common tasks. A remote model can still receive the raw inference query after adaptation via a privatized artifact [47]. 

4.2 User-Level Units and Empirical Risk Rewards in Parameter Updates 

Parameter updates embed private data directly into weights or adapters invoked across unbounded future tasks, creating an observable surface distinct from prompt releases. Mind the Privacy Unit! [22] evaluates both Group Privacy (Algorithm 1, capping per-user records under example-level DP-SGD) and User-wise DP-SGD (Algorithms 2–3, per-user gradient clipping and Gaussian noise) for LoRA fine-tuning. Black-box (𝜀, 𝛿 ) group conversion over 𝑘 correlated records scales 𝜀 by 𝑘 and inflates 𝛿 

exponentially, whereas per-user clipping and RDP accounting avoid that superlinear 𝛿 blow-up. Still, bounding one participant’s aggregate contribution in a multi-user corpus differs structurally from hiding a personal fact inside one user’s persistent model. Moreover, random-reshuffling epochs diverge from idealized Poisson subsampling assumed by the accountant, and incremental steps, replay, and cross-task allocation alter accounting premises. Unvalidated for online single-user writeback, this work is coded T.Private parameter adaptation varies optimization cost and deployment scale. DP zeroth-order fine-tuning [108] privatizes loss differences under example adjacency, avoiding per-sample gradient storage. Production Gboard [134] uses tree-based DP-FTRL, per-device clipping, and participation Privacy Protection in Self-Evolving Personal Agents 9

limits. Slokom and Bellogín [100] deploy clipping and Gaussian perturbation before server aggre-gation. Their 53-day study measures recommendation use and user control, not privacy outcomes, and does not specify adjacency or continual accounting. We retain it as deployment context without a protection code. Contribution privacy does not ensure that adapted weights preserve contextual disclosure norms. 

Privacy Collapse [40] tests held-out tool and memory behavior after offline fine-tuning. Norm-based filtering and conservative data mixing partly mitigate the regression (§5.4, App. I; T). The result concerns release behavior, not the confidentiality of the training records. Earlier private online personalization also separates the shared update from the local policy. P2B [81] sends sampled, encoded interaction feedback through a trusted shuffler that suppresses rare context codes. The server uses the filtered batch to train a shared warm-start model. The pub-lished analysis uses one-context-vector adjacency for this sharing step. Collaborative bandits [117] perturb sufficient statistics under reward-step adjacency and analyze the action transcript. Both are transferable evidence ( T). Extending either to a private fact repeated over one user’s history requires accounting for all affected interactions and releases. Privacy-preserving BERT [90] applies metric 𝑑 𝜒 -privacy ( 𝑒 𝜂 𝑑 (𝑥,𝑥 ′ ) ) before transmitting token or text representations and recovers utility through privacy-adaptive pretraining. The inverse-distance units of 𝜂 make its budget specific to the embedding metric. Repeated releases can carry semantically and temporally related signals. Underlying these methods is a modeling asymmetry between cohort training and single-user per-sonalization. Federated user-DP defines adjacency by adding or removing one participant’s dataset; in an agent serving one user longitudinally, removing that sole user eliminates the personalization target itself. When the same mechanism admits an empty intermediate history and observes the same outputs, add/remove-user (𝜀, 𝛿 )-DP implies (2𝜀, (1 + 𝑒 𝜀 )𝛿 )-DP for replacing that user’s history by a remove-then-add argument. A particular fact change is a special case. The cohort result still needs separate checks of its input domain, output scope, and utility in a continuously adapting personal agent. The isolation boundary also differs from the noise mechanism. PRISP compares private per-user adaptation using a shared task-conditioned anchor, with the learned personal module kept separate[88]. Its few-shot LaMP experiments use fixed histories. A provider that receives neither the user history nor the trained module lacks those direct inputs. When a recipient can query the adapted model, extraction and membership tests assess a different boundary. Du et al. compare those outcomes across DP fine-tuning methods and show that the empirical privacy–utility trade-off varies with the adaptation method[29]. Parameter audits must also test sensitive details confined to training inputs rather than target completions. Extraction probing of fine-tuned language models [104] compares DP, machine unlearning, regularization, and alignment against input-side PII probes, showing that audits must verify extractability of input-only facts, cross-lingual identifiers, and rare personal values. PrivMeSA [116] uses empirical disclosure risk to optimize a shared clinical LoRA with GRPO across 1,700 training patients (§§3.3–4.1). A separate frozen distiller writes general lessons from completed consultations. During test-time adaptation the policy is fixed and the initially empty lesson store grows across 897 encounters from 200 held-out patients. The exposure metrics concern outbound consultations and registry-based linkage across visits. The training, writeback, and release results all receive T: the evaluated state is shared across patients, and linking visits measures disclosure about a patient. An intra-user parameter claim would require an adapter updated from that user’s own history and a test of the interfaces that expose its changes. 10 Zibin Lin, Taotao Wang, and Shengli Zhang 

Formal DP and empirical risk rewards constrain distinct objects. User-level DP bounds single-participant influence on released parameters while trusting a central trainer and exact clipping, sampling, and accounting; empirical risk rewards steer optimization away from evaluator-detected disclosures. Combining them inside a personal agent requires joint data-dependency analysis across risk-reward updates, lesson distillation, and replay under a shared privacy budget. PeCL [143] adds a task-incremental comparison ( T). It perturbs token embeddings and uses sensitivity scores to guide retention as parameters change. Appendix G calibrates Gaussian noise at a specified scale for a token-level unit. Section 3.2 perturbs only tokens with nonzero sensitivity scores, so a neighboring input can change whether noise is added. In a personal history, one fact may change multiple tokens, contextual features, and the scale or retention decision itself. Transferring the intervention requires an adjacency and a bound that covers these changes and the full set of releases. A fixed-compute comparison shows how example and user sampling change utility under user adjacency [9]. PriME uses membership tests on donor adapters in an empirically optimized merge. Its target user and protected donors are distinct [61]. 

4.3 Preference Protection: Complete Tuples, Choice Labels, and Single-Shot Sampling 

Preference alignment must distinguish its protection unit and remain separate from disclosure-preference gates (Section 5.2). Decoupled DP reward modeling [19] protects the complete compara-tive tuple (prompt, response_1, response_2, preference) : a trusted trainer fits a lightweight DP reward head, and Best-of-𝑁 selection scores candidates from a public reference model. Post-processing immunity (Proposition 6) holds only when both the candidate generator 𝜋 0 and the reward head’s frozen representation backbone 𝜙 are strictly data-independent and raw tuples are never re-accessed; unseen private user queries and retraining lie outside this static bound ( T). DP reward estimation [91] adopts narrower label-DP (central objective perturbation by a trusted learner or user-side local randomized response), treating prompts and candidate actions as public (T). When sensitive facts reside in prompts or candidate responses, hiding choice labels leaves the primary leakage surface open; conversely, full-tuple mechanisms impose broader trust and noise costs when only choices require protection. Nominal 𝜀 values across label-DP and tuple-DP are therefore incomparable unless protection units match. PrivDPO [54] encodes label-DP directly into DPO policy updates by adding calibrated randomness along a text-determined 1D preference axis. This boundary assumes public prompts and responses plus an in-distribution reference policy 𝜋 ref that is data-independent or privately pre-trained (e.g., via PrivSFT); non-private SFT leaves prompt and response memorization exposed. Sparse reward estimation [26] jointly analyzes Sample-DP and randomized-response label-DP in sparse Bradley– Terry–Luce models, characterizing estimation error across granularities while treating prompts and candidates as public. Recent offline results enlarge the preference comparison. Square 𝜒 PO analyzes offline policy fitting under either central sample privacy or local label privacy [154]. A rank-aware release samples a binary top subset under local privacy for a single item relocation in a fixed candidate ranking [12]. A private fact can change multiple positions, several lists, or the candidate set itself. Each change requires matching the ranking mechanism’s domain and charging the applicable group and repeated releases. KL-regularized bandits and RLHF [148] distinguish a private action from a private policy. Their pure label-DP result covers one draw from an offline KL-regularized policy under reward, coverage, and boundedness conditions (Theorem 3). Repeated draws require accounting, and the parameters lie outside that bound. Wu et al. [127] instead privatize each choice label by randomized response at the client before adaptive reward fitting and policy updates (Section 5, Algorithm 2). Their online Privacy Protection in Self-Evolving Personal Agents 11 

result is a regret bound under label LDP. The experiments evaluate the offline algorithm. For a remote learner that must never see raw choices, local privatization protects the label interface even during online learning. Repeated privatizations of a fact-linked label, private contexts and candidates, and other recipient-visible releases require a separate analysis. DPPrefSyn generates synthetic comparisons from public prompts after private reward fitting [36]. PrivAct evaluates contextual disclosure during agent tasks after shared offline preference train-ing [17]. 

4.4 Boundaries of Confidential Execution and Continual Composition 

4.4.1 Secure Execution, Federated Sharing, and Permitted Outputs. Outside differential privacy, SecP-Tuning [77] executes secret-shared forward passes of a frozen backbone across two semi-honest, non-colluding online servers while the data owner keeps labels, losses, and CMA-ES soft-prompt updates local ( T). Offline preprocessing relies on a trusted third-party dealer 𝑇 (collusion with either online server breaks secrecy), MPC security covers an approximated fixed-point Random Feature Attention circuit rather than exact Softmax, and forward logit predictions are reconstructed in plaintext to the client across tuning iterations. Its three-A100 LAN/WAN benchmarks quantify cryptographic overhead, leaving allowed-output leakage, user-level adjacency, and consumer-device feasibility unaddressed. Federated mechanisms separate upload secrecy from output leakage. Personalized federated prompt learning (DP-FPL) [111] decomposes local soft prompts into client-local personalized residuals retained on-device, uploaded local low-rank factors protected by local DP (LDP), and an aggregated global prompt protected by global DP (GDP)—still requiring continual accounting across participation schedules ( T). Practical Secure Aggregation [7] hides individual updates prior to summation, subject to threshold non-collusion ( < 𝑡 corrupted parties), authenticated PKI binding against active Sybil attacks, and cross-round cohort-differencing leakage when participating sets shift by one client; aggregate updates and derived models remain permitted outputs. The choice of federated prompt mechanism changes what the server learns. SecFPP prevents direct access to prompt vectors during clustering but discloses distances, whose information content the authors analyze under a Gaussian model [51]. FedSEPT accounts for noisy prompt updates across communication rounds under its sampler and clipping conditions [122]. Both evaluate vision–language settings. For a personal prompt, the comparison turns on whether the server observes a private update, a clustering statistic, or both. A distance based on a private prompt belongs in that observer’s view even when the underlying vectors stay hidden. 

4.4.2 Continual Release, Adaptive Accounting, and Stopping Conditions. Continual updates require separating single-mechanism structure from cross-mechanism composition. Lifelong DP [63] uses objective perturbation and prescribed reuse of fixed, disjoint episodic task datasets or task batches to prove a pure 𝜀 -Lifelong-DP bound ( 𝛿 = 0) across released checkpoints ( T). Correlation among distinct task tuples leaves the stated tuple-level guarantee intact. A fact-level change spanning several tuples or task partitions requires separate accounting under that adjacency. Replay outside the protocol can violate its partition or data-access assumptions, and a separately released raw-data summary lies outside the protected checkpoint view. Concurrent composition [49] models adaptively interleaved queries and dataset updates over full transcripts: while approximate DP ( 𝛿 > 0) forfeits naive parallel composition under adaptive updates, pure (𝜀, 0)-DP preserves concurrent parallel composition (Corollary 5.5), previewing the pure-DP memory design in Section 5.2. Neither framework confers formal privacy onto empirical gates or skill writebacks. For an explicitly modeled correlated history, inferential privacy offers a different guarantee: compare release distributions conditioned on alternative secret values. Repeated updates require specifying adjacency units, fresh data access, and release composition. 

Chaudhuri [101] give composition results for Markov Quilt under specified time-series conditions. Bai et al. [5] characterize additional conditions for linear adaptive Pufferfish composition. These results require assumptions about both the mechanism and the history model. Their applicability to feedback-dependent personal-agent traces needs a separate argument. Recent accountants better match adaptive workflows under strict preconditions. Single-pass tree DP-FTRL [56] avoids sampling or shuffling amplification but fails under unprescribed multi-epoch replay without multi-participation matrix or tree accounting. Fully adaptive composition [124] selects subsequent mechanisms and budgets from prior outputs, pairing privacy filters (enforcing a pre-committed global (𝜀, 𝛿 ) cap) with odometers (paying an 𝑂 (√︁ log log (·)) time-uniformity penalty over fixed schedules). Continual mean estimation [39] proves user-level pure DP across multi-user cohorts ( 𝑀 𝑡 ≪ 𝑡 ); in a single-user agent ( 𝑢 = 1, 𝑀 𝑡 = 𝑡 ), whole-user replacement alters 100% of data and forces Ω(1) error unless re-instantiated at bounded intra-user event, window, or attribute granularity. PRISM privatizes client adapter updates, whereas CSLR privatizes replay-list release. Their guarantees concern separate outputs within multi-client learning [98, 123]. Figure 2 compares representative mechanisms. Applying their bounds to personal trajectories requires matching each operand to the same history change and tracking all resulting releases. 

5 Memory Writeback, Dynamic Maintenance, Withdrawal, and Read Release 

Long-term memory in a personal agent spans raw interaction logs, user profiles, vector indices, episodic summaries, reflections, and retrieval caches. Ingestion, compression, merging, expiration, retrieval, and subsequent writeback continually alter both state representation and propagation Privacy Protection in Self-Evolving Personal Agents 13 

scope. Coverage across these lifecycle stages remains uneven: pre-write isolation, enclave-backed maintenance, and empirical writeback gates supply direct personal-agent update evidence ( D), and read-time release admits formal differential privacy ( S), while physical erasure, derived-state cleanup, and synchronized parameter withdrawal remain uncertified in long-horizon loops. Offensive benchmarks confirm that storage isolation and retrieval accuracy do not establish confidentiality. MEXTRA [114] extracts private interactions from two agent memory architectures under black-box prompting, and SPORE [38] exploits malicious tool responses to exfiltrate user-isolated memories via tool-call arguments and persist trigger payloads across sessions. Memory protection must therefore govern model outputs, tool parameters, cross-session short-term state, and external execution transcripts alongside storage permissions. 

5.1 Writeback Boundaries: Representation Isolation versus Selective Admission 

Writeback control balances personal-context utility against persistent retention through two strate-gies: replacing raw values with isolated surrogates or gating whether an item enters persistent storage. Representation isolation relies on separation between raw values and placeholders, while se-lective admission relies on sensitivity classification; both require specifying which trusted endpoint reads raw inputs during the decision. MemPrivacy [16] detects sensitive text spans on a trusted edge device and substitutes typed placeholders prior to cloud memory upload while keeping the recovery map local. SP-Mem [120] separates sanitized default retrieval views from exact private values, restoring exact values only under task necessity and explicit consent. Both writeback interventions are coded D. The retrieval and restoration path in SP-Mem has a separate scope, with release conditional on consent enforce-ment. Placeholder replacement still leaves attribute inference from co-occurring placeholders or agent actions open, and SP-Mem’s validation relies on synthetic personas and simulated consent. As an adjacent RAG primitive ( T), SAGE [142] replaces private documents with synthetic corpora before indexing, yet requires a trusted synthesizer and re-identification audits over generated text. HealthJev [65] tests whether simulated longitudinal health interactions admit restricted attributes, procedures, or events to per-user memory ( D). ConMem [147] probes the downstream effects of adding a candidate to reconstructed conversations. Its casewise storage judgments quantify over-retention at admission, with repeated writes and confidentiality outside that evaluation ( T). The two interventions help distinguish which facts may be written from how a stored fact should be used or disclosed. Longitudinal evidence changes the comparison between entry and release controls. Kaneko et al. [57] compare anonymizing summaries at write time with instructing a support agent to avoid stating or presupposing attributes unless the user raises them. In their simulated multi-session loop, both interventions lowered measured attribute inference from the accumulated record; the response instruction preserved more coherence. It changes later elicitation and thus affects what reaches memory, while write-time anonymization directly changes what is stored. These are empirical results under the tested user models and memory formats. Dash et al. [25] audit 2,050 memory entries donated by 80 ChatGPT users and separately evaluate pre-ingest query reformulation using a memory-generation proxy. This motivates testing the write boundary on deployed systems and leaves the reformulation’s longitudinal effect open. Agent-Memory Protocol [126] provides design context through worked examples of at-rest redaction, purpose-scoped release, and local restoration. These examples specify the interfaces at which retention and disclosure would be tested. AIM [55] assigns visibility before writing and enforces per-user scope at a shared vector index (T). Audience-Bound Persistent Memory [75] binds a user’s records to their original audiences and assigns an intersection or suppresses the derivative when source audiences differ. Controlled multi-party histories exercise these ingest and derivation operations ( D). Context admission has its 14 Zibin Lin, Taotao Wang, and Shengli Zhang 

own authorization evidence ( S). The guarantees depend on trusted identity resolution, provenance, and complete mediation. An authorized context may still produce revealing actions, and byte erasure lies outside the claim. Cross-session linkage gives a second reason to evaluate the read boundary. MemJoin [136] follows 288 synthetic cases through six persistent memory systems with a common reader. A sensitive relation may be materialized during writing or inferred only after retrieval joins records. On 287 paired A-Mem cases, masking values in the retrieved context reduced case disclosure from 13.9% to 0% and task completion from 20.2% to 5.2%. Removing shared cues before the reader reduced disclosure to 5.6% with 19.2% completion and increased refusal from 7.2% to 14.1% (Table 3). Both interventions act on the read view. These results motivate testing whether admission and write-time masking leave linkable traces across records, alongside whether the read view can preserve task-relevant values while suppressing unnecessary links. Retention policies act on already-admitted state. MaRS/FiFA couples typed provenance and sensitivity-aware eviction in multi-agent simulations. The study found no significant privacy-score differences across policies despite different utility and cost, and its DP tie-break fired infrequently [2]. FSFM measures residual sensitive-record retention in a storage benchmark and leaves recovery through answers and descendants untested [42]. Both are T for maintenance. CAMS combines write screening, ongoing re-audit, and extraction interception against a shared test harness ( T); its extraction results cannot be attributed to write screening alone [105]. HomeLLaMA constrains the update boundary by keeping user profiles and home configuration local ( D) [53]. Approved plans and commands become interaction records. A local update inserts or merges profile entries and later retrieves them for action planning (§IV-C–D). In the 25-day lab study, ten participants provided feedback and rated personalization, and the study tracked optional cloud calls (§VII-B, Fig. 16). The cloud path receives the current request after obfuscation and user approval. The profile boundary limits direct transmission. The separate fallback experiment measures inference about current commands under its own adversary (§VI-B.2, T). The local model’s offline training uses generic device tasks. The observed longitudinal personalization comes from the evolving local profile path. 

5.2 Dynamic Maintenance and Read Release: Confidential Execution Is Not Update Privacy 

5.2.1 Confidential Maintenance and Oblivious Indexing. Dynamic maintenance can expose access patterns, update frequencies, and state rollbacks. Opal [58] uses a trusted logical client holding a root key and durable monotonic counter (𝑘, ctr ) (D). An attested controller authenticates two oblivious RAM (ORAM) trees via Merkle roots and counter-bound MACs. It coordinates trusted embedding and LLM enclaves and performs ingestion, index maintenance, expiration, and summarization within fixed access budgets. Theorem 2.1 bounds host observations for request sequences with the same declared leakage, including public operation types and checkpoint schedules. Intra-enclave side channels and denial of service fall outside that model. The freshness result for sealed state ends at the latest authenticated checkpoint. Expiry reclaims records, while retained summaries have their own lifecycle. Compass [155] co-designs HNSW search with a Ring ORAM variant and requires no server-side TEE ( T). Opal implements its own Ring ORAM variant with an IVF-PQ index and uses Compass’s batched-access ORAM results for trace indistinguishability and authenticated-storage integrity (§5 and App. A of Opal [58]). Oblix [83] uses an SGX enclave with doubly oblivious indexing to address page and cache side channels ( T). Each guarantee applies to its storage or search interface. Model and tool disclosures require separate controls. Privacy Protection in Self-Evolving Personal Agents 15 

Post-deletion index confidentiality requires separate specification. Symmetric puncturable en-cryption [103] realizes Type-III backward security ( T): it hides deleted document identifiers only for keyword–document pairs never queried while active, while still leaking update timestamps and exact Delete -to-Insert cancellation pairs on later searches. Client-cached copies, model contexts, vector summaries, and parameter traces remain outside this storage-interface bound. 

5.2.2 Private Retrieval, Context Release, and Tool Information Flow. DP-MemView [60] selects from public views by the exponential mechanism, charges all attribute groups intersecting the private read set, and falls back to a generic view when a cap would be exceeded. Under its interface contract and fixed layout, Theorem 1 gives pure 𝐵 𝑎 -DP for changes confined to the indexed group 𝐼 𝑎 . A changed slot outside 𝐼 𝑎 falls outside that bound. When groups cover all changed slots, Corollary 1 sums the budgets of a minimum-cost cover. An uncovered change receives no finite bound from that corollary. A dynamically rewritten layout needs its own coverage and transcript analysis ( S

for the evaluated read interface). Profile-DP [67] uses a fixed profile and sharing policy ( T). DP-SLOT samples fields independently, and DP-BLOCK jointly samples related fields on public support. Theorem 1 and Appendix A give a pure-DP bound for withheld values with fixed public context and field sets. Reusing a cached sanitized profile with public inputs preserves the bound. Fresh sanitizations compose. MemGov [21] checks lifecycle status and requester eligibility, then repairs pairwise conflicts before context assembly. Its certificate verifies the supplied constraints on the returned working set (§§3–4 and App. D). Private RAG designs decouple query, retrieval, and generation endpoints ( T). RemoteRAG [18] applies metric DistanceDP (𝑒 𝜂 𝑑 (· ,·) ) to outbound query embeddings; because 𝜂 carries inverse-distance units scaling distinguishability exponentially with embedding distance and additively across queries, nominal budgets are non-comparable to dimensionless 𝜀 -DP. PRAG [68] computes homomorphic similarity scores under passive, semi-honest IND-CPA assumptions in non-interactive (PRAG-I ) or client-assisted interactive ( PRAG-II ) modes, leaving active probing, server access patterns, and decrypted generation contexts to separate protection. Galaxy [6] integrates a Persona cognitive tree, a self-evolving Kernel, and a Privacy Gate that applies graded L1–L4 masking before cloud calls and selective restoration upon return. Because its PrivacyLens ablation removes the entire Kernel rather than isolating the Privacy Gate—and never proves that all internal Persona or Kernel updates pass through that gate—its evidence supports empirical release gating ( S) rather than protected state evolution ( D). AirGapAgent [4] separates a trusted minimizer from the third-party-facing agent ( S). Its single-turn context-hijacking benchmark on synthetic profiles reports a 97% protection rate with Gemini Ultra. Contextual integrity studies [1] identify data type, recipient, purpose, and transmission prin-ciple as factors in release decisions. For personalized decisions, PANOLA [113] negotiates sharing through learned bids, and the Uncertainty-Aware Personal Assistant defers under uncertainty [3]. ARIEL [34] uses logical entailment over prior user judgments to assess a new request, with an undetermined output when entailed judgments have no majority. Its benchmarks measure decision agreement under non-adversarial requests ( S). Evaluating compliance at release and protecting the judgment history during preference updates require distinct tests. CIMemories varies tasks and recipients to test disclosure appropriateness, and HUSH-Bench distinguishes retrieval of a target from its unsolicited use even when the exact record is absent [82, 132]. MemGate uses a query-conditioned gate before the reader in scripted memory-agent tests [146]. In a fixed-pool study, Liu [72] isolates stricter context admission from presentation of already admitted content. Stricter admission reduces measured cross-domain leakage in one matched configuration. Utility losses and the lack of a robust difference between the two tested renderings 16 Zibin Lin, Taotao Wang, and Shengli Zhang 

limit extrapolation. Even a response that omits sensitive text can expose preferences through the choices it makes. In UMPeek, the evaluated gate compares personalized and non-personalized choices and withholds output as evidence accumulates. Across repeated requests it lowers measured semantic recovery, with residual disclosure and task-score costs (Section VII, Tables IV–V) [66]. The result supports evaluating recipient-visible decisions alongside retrieved evidence and task utility. Execution-time gates operate outside the model context window. FLOWSEAL [99] enforces lattice provenance with controlled declassification over tool disclosures, and PrivacyFlow [102] weighs purpose, recipient prior knowledge, and inferential consequences before execution to minimize, block, or confirm actions. Securing Agents with Tracked Capabilities [85] constrains the agent to generating statically typed Scala 3 code in which confidential values use local pure functions and preserve Classified wrappers. Static capability typing blocks explicit plaintext exfiltration over tracked channels under compiler, runtime, and local-model trust. However, it excludes timing and termination covert channels, implicit control-flow branching or declassification (selecting tools from sanitized predicates), and compiler or runtime exception messages returned to an untrusted cloud model across retry turns. All three mechanisms are coded T rather than S because they enforce general per-task tool or program information-flow policies over transient execution inputs rather than gating reads or releases from a persistent longitudinal personal-agent memory store. Delegation and audit can create additional recipients. Task-Graph CI evaluates propagation gates across several agents; PiSAs measures cross-user visibility as well as inappropriate transfers in shared workflows [45, 125]. Field-tier minimization limits which raw action parameters enter the authorization and audit views [86]. These results concern different views and do not bound what the final tool recipient learns. Client-side draft modification uses retained local profiles and audits inference from query and response. RAG-CT instead tests blocking against PII extraction from fixed retrieval corpora [78, 95]. 

5.3 Withdrawal and Derived States: From Behavioral Invalidation to Synchronized Unlearning 

Withdrawal mechanisms target distinct outcomes. In Reglos [137], the design masks selected identifiers before ingest. A conversational forget request appends a tombstone, and the reader policy instructs the model to avoid the retracted fact. The PersonaMem-v2 outcome observes non-use after the combined intervention. The UI can also delete selected entries. Claims about summaries, backups, embeddings, or weights need separate evidence. Shen et al. [97] independently test a retrieval guard for revoked organizational policies. Native writeback can leave descendants that continue to affect decisions even when the revoked entry is blocked (Appendix C.1). This is evidence of behavioral persistence under policy revocation ( T), with sensitive-fact erasure outside its scope. Two benchmarks expose residual risks after source deletion. Deployment-time memorization analysis [13] introduces the Forgetting Residue Score across summarization strength, retrieval breadth, and deletion modes, while MemCut [144] separates native retrieval recovery from direct reconstruction out of surviving memory objects. Both confirm that deleting explicit records leaves summaries and dispersed fragments sufficient for secret reconstruction. AfterDeleteBench [93] adds a matched never-exposed control and tests retirement or rebuild of descendants under complete recorded lineage. Its fixed-weight synthetic harness isolates a supported memory path and leaves learned dependencies and incomplete traces open. Memora [112] detects reliance on superseded facts during later tasks. Its forgetting-aware accuracy measures behavioral use of obsolete state. Bemula [129] adds no-request controls and measures preservation of unrelated facts separately from target forgetting. AUBench [70] expands the outcome to residual knowledge in parameters, memory, and tool-mediated reacquisition. Privacy Protection in Self-Evolving Personal Agents 17 

Synchronized Backflow Unlearning (SBU) [115] coordinates memory and parameter cleanup. It blocks retrieval via a target blocklist, prunes exclusive provenance closures over dependency graphs while logically invalidating shared multi-source artifacts, and suppresses parametric residue on 

D𝐹 via KL divergence alignment toward a high-entropy reference distribution—a behavioral logit-flattening objective rather than weight-space erasure. In its Store–Query–Delete–Probe medical QA loop, forget-set retrieval hit rate drops from 100% to 0%, supplemented by backflow diagnostics and ablations. Because evaluation uses public medical QA without single-user longitudinal histories, shared artifacts remain logically invalidated, multi-agent provenance graphs can miss implicit flows, and retrieval, MIA, and QA scores remain empirical indicators, SBU is coded T.Secure Forgetting [139] targets state, trajectory, or environment knowledge and tests target behavior against retained tasks and a never-exposed comparator on public tasks ( T). This com-plements SBU’s joint memory and parameter intervention: the comparator can expose residual behavior, while cleanup requires inspecting affected persistent state. External RAG knowledge-base editing [119] likewise yields behavioral forgetting while leaving base weights intact. Formal base-lines define sharper targets ( T). Certified data removal [44] requires 𝐿 2-regularized strong convexity, Lipschitz-Hessian smoothness, secret linear objective perturbation at initial training, curator gradi-ent and Hessian access, and bounded cumulative deletions. Crucially, its (𝜀, 𝛿 ) guarantee bounds only the marginal post-removal distribution 𝜃 − and leaves the joint pair (𝜃 ∗, 𝜃 − ) exposed if 𝜃 ∗

was queried without DP. Verifiable machine unlearning [32] uses initial Proof-of-Training state commitments, SNARKs, and hash chains to prove computational integrity of execution, yet does not establish statistical non-memorization when wrapping approximate rules. Deletion-as-control [23] rejects confidentiality-based deletion in favor of counterfactual post-deletion control and adaptive history-independence simulation conditioned on pre-deletion transcripts. Gupta et al. [46] treat requests chosen in response to previously released models and reduce adaptive unlearning to suitable non-adaptive guarantees. Chourasia and Shah [20] show that equivalence to retraining alone can still leave deleted values inferable under such requests. Their post-deletion privacy target covers a sequence of releases. Applying these guarantees across an agent’s coupled summaries, indices, skills, backups, and weights remains open. Unlearning also exposes an interface conflict between executing deletion and releasing post-deletion models. Differential leakage analysis [14] shows that querying pre- and post-unlearning versions amplifies membership inference over querying the original model alone, evaluating formal differential privacy separately from empirical output coarseners (label-only release and temperature scaling). Conversely, TAPE [121] uses pre-/post-unlearning posterior differences, shadow models, and reconstructors to audit unlearning without backdoors ( T). Coarsening outputs to label-only predictions suppresses the posterior vectors needed by black-box TAPE auditing, requiring withdrawal interfaces to specify version access, query budgets, and output granularity jointly. EFU [84] addresses request privacy ( T). Applying functional encryption to round-level aggrega-tions requires a trusted key-generation authority (KGA) or interactive distributed key generation (DKG) plus at least two non-colluding clients; EFU cryptographically hides pre-aggregation cipher-texts and prevents selective decryption by a compliant server. Hiding learning-versus-unlearning intent in decrypted aggregates, however, relies on heuristic parameter-importance and adversarial-example losses rather than a cryptographic output-indistinguishability proof, leaving intra-agent dependency cleanup uncovered; Xu et al. [131] situate these targets and verification modes within machine unlearning broadly. TimlMemBench supplies revocation and preservation probes after native ingestion [153]. Figure 3 aligns twelve representative mechanisms across the six memory-lifecycle interfaces. 6 Admission Protection in Skill and Experience Evolution 

Skills convert episodic experience into reusable procedural policies invoked across future tasks. ExpeL [150] shows how distilling natural-language insights and trajectories drives persistent capability gains, while AntiSkillBench [128] demonstrates that persona skills distilled from private trajectories concentrate sensitive personal facts, enable attribute inference, and facilitate behavioral impersonation. We organize skill-evolution protections across candidate generation, evaluation, persistent admission, and execution, tracing where raw private inputs are read, which artifacts leave the device, and whether admitted skills remain compliant over time. 

6.1 Candidate Generation and Evaluation: The Evaluator Is Also a Recipient 

Skill privacy involves both the input to distillation and the admission of its output. AntiSkill-Bench [128] tests sanitizing dialogue before distillation and perturbing the synthesized skill ( T). CounterPersona [118] intervenes on newly authored records to limit fidelity of unauthorized distil-lation over a retained archive ( T); the original records remain available to the distiller. P2Skill [92] refines reusable cloud-local routing and redaction instructions on benchmark queries ( T). Its runtime leakage measurements concern cloud-bound subtasks, while its offline cloud evaluator can see the original refinement prompt. These cases expose different private inputs at the distiller, evaluator, and runtime interfaces. MaskClaw [152] provides transferable evidence ( T) for candidate skill filtering in five designed scenarios. Section 4.5 specifies a local audit-and-merge update. E3 instead reports fixed correction Privacy Protection in Self-Evolving Personal Agents 19 

inputs, text-scorer and sandbox outcomes, and held-out behavior tests with the arbitration store fixed (§§5.5, 6.3–6.4, Apps. E–F). No reported E3 trace links a committed skill version to retrieval and use in a subsequent user interaction. The external multimodal judge is a separate recipient (App. K, Table 28); the 832-sample static GUI arbitration evaluation does not test persistent updates. Federated frameworks enforce payload filters at the sharing boundary. FedSkillGit [64] applies a client-side sanitizer and private-value auditor before committing skill patches or summaries to a version-controlled repository, while FederatedSkill [135] distills local trajectories into compact semantic diffs audited for entity leakage, task reconstruction, and PII canaries before aggregation. Both rely on empirical content filtering for multi-client shared skills and leave a single user’s longi-tudinal library unaddressed, earning code T. Moreover, passing payload audits leaves submission metadata—committer identity, timestamps, and accepted revisions—visible to the coordinating server. Personal admission and federated commits therefore share sanitization primitives under distinct observer and retention models. Zep Agent Skills documents a trace-to-version pipeline that scans candidates for instance data, limits compilation to the namespace, and retires dependent skills from search after source dele-tion [8]. The evaluation runs the admitted snapshot and reports task outcomes. Its privacy controls supply design evidence at the admission and withdrawal boundaries. SkillLadder evaluates entity and lineage scrubbing before synchronizing skills across simulated users. Covered entity retention reaches zero at its highest scrub level, while source attribution remains above chance [15]. 

6.2 Persistent Admission and Execution: Permission Constraints Beyond Content Audits 

Even sanitized skills may harvest fresh personal data or invoke unauthorized tools during execution. SKIPPER [130] exposes widespread mismatches between declared privacy policies and black-box runtime collection across third-party personal assistant apps, while static tracked capabilities [85] (Section 5.2) enforce lexical capability permissions and Classified propagation constraints in generated code. SKIPPER supplies risk evidence and tracked capabilities provide an execution primitive, yet neither protects candidate distillation from private failure traces; self-generated personal skills require jointly verifying skill text, declared recipients, and runtime tool invocations. SkillJack tests both candidate review and runtime monitoring on reused benchmark skills [140]. Its exploratory results show that adding a review gate can change which skill is selected and which actions the runtime monitor must handle. Trigger rates are policy-violation proxies rather than disclosure rates, and no private longitudinal user state is protected in the experiment ( T). SkillGuard tests runtime permission mediation on reusable benchmark skills [87]. Its effects depend on manifest coverage and the agent scaffold, and the experiments record both blocked vio-lations and task completion. SkillLifeBench separates the selected version from the implementation actually invoked and links that evidence to recovery [145]. Its paired recovery experiments show improved joint task and authorization outcomes when the diagnoser sees execution provenance. Both supply T evidence for verifying reuse. They motivate checking the loaded implementation as well as the version stored in the registry. Privacy rules and skill contracts also evolve longitudinally. PrivAgentFlow [107] updates work-flow privacy policies from historical failures ( T), Skill-V [79] pairs versioned skill libraries with falsifiable contracts and replay-based non-regression checks, and experience-faithfulness eval-uations [151] verify whether stored experiences causally govern downstream decisions or are bypassed at inference time. Integrating these mechanisms requires logging provenance links and admission rationales per revision, enforcing permission invariants at invocation, and re-testing affected paths after fact withdrawal or rollback (Section 5.3). For the evaluated admission gate, runtime permission enforcement and post-withdrawal skill invalidation remain separate outcomes. 20 Zibin Lin, Taotao Wang, and Shengli Zhang 

7 Cross-Carrier Comparison of Privacy Boundaries 7.1 Matching a Fact, an Observer, and a Claim 

Follow one private constraint—evening appointments are unsuitable—through booking, learning, and withdrawal. It is present in 𝐻 1 and absent in 𝐻 0. In each scenario, fix public tasks, options, and permissions; map the change to inputs and derived artifacts; and specify the observer’s view. These are worked comparisons, not runs of an integrated agent. Measurements come from component studies or Supplementary Section S4. Table 4 marks results as transferable when the input and view match, out of scope when they do not, and insufficient when the match is unverified. This complements authority and recovery checks: even an authorized action can distinguish private histories [10, 27, 71]. 

Sensitivity to the evidence rule. The bounded primary-source rescreen (Supplementary Sec-tion S6.5) retained nine scoped anchors and upheld three boundary exclusions. Requiring measured outcomes after state carryover leaves just two direct comparators in this set: reader non-use and tracked-memory withdrawal. Booking and preference guarantees remain conditional transfers, and skill admission remains a proxy. No checked source evaluates the linked lifecycle view. The rescreen does not reconstruct Stage 2 or estimate coverage beyond these records. 

7.2 Scenario 1: Book an Appointment 

Transfer to linked recipients. 𝐻 1 adds one training and one validation example and changes a preallocated memory value for attribute 𝑎 . The linked observer sees the selected prompt, app transcript, and booking exchange; raw inputs, candidates, and scores stay local. The public calendar has two daytime slots and one evening slot. Stable voting groups with full sampling in a DP-OPT variant limit each fixed-prefix change to one replaced ballot [50]. Calibrating for a removal followed by an insertion gives per-call (𝜀 0, 𝛿 0) (Supplementary Section S6.1). Count all 𝐾 allowed private calls, including retries and rejections. Conditional on the candidate list, validation-count selection costs 𝜀 𝑣 ; conditional on the selected prompt, DP-MemView gives pure 𝐵 𝑎 -DP for reads on the common scaffold [60]. The same fact reaches both prompt stages, so sequential composition gives 

(𝐾𝜀 0 + 𝜀 𝑣 + 𝐵 𝑎 , 𝐾𝛿 0).

This bound extends to requests, responses, timing, and errors generated only from those protected outputs and public inputs. The worked policy chooses between the two daytime slots in both histories. 

7.3 Scenario 1 Continued: Change the Action or Observer 

If no valid request is for evening in 𝐻 1, DP implies Pr [evening | 𝐻 0] ≤ 𝛿 . A raw-data controller that always requests evening in 𝐻 0 therefore needs 𝛿 ≥ 1 [35]. This is an analytical limit on usable slots, not a measured rate. An extra host-visible ingest only in 𝐻 1 likewise forces 𝛿 ≥ 1 for a linked host view. Opal compares traces with equal declared leakage. Matching operation counts removes one signal; the full host view still needs a conditional simulation or compatible separate bound [58]. 

Measured proxy trade-off. On synthetic PairedMem with the same Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct backbone and 16 fixed queries, DP-MemView Table 2 reports (transcript AUC, target-required utility) of 

(0.832 , 0.524 ) for TaskMin, (0.500 , 0.386 ) for TypedMask, and (0.490 , 0.506 ) for online DP-MemView (𝐵 𝑎 = 2). TypedMask gives chance-level AUC by construction. Relative to TaskMin, the online interface lowers the auditor’s measured distinguishability with a smaller utility loss than typed masking [60]. 7.4 Scenario 2: Learn from Feedback and Reuse a Skill 

From a memory fact to a preference head. With the same two offered plans, a flipped preference fits label adjacency; changed candidate text requires tuple accounting, including all affected tuples. Reranking inherits the bound only if the generator and online inputs use no unaccounted history [19, 91]. Neither local adapter isolation nor cohort DP for released weights covers raw examples supplied to a training provider [22, 106]. Section 7.7 tests the utility cost of protecting more labels. 

From feedback to an executable skill. Skill content and actions inherit the private head’s bound only if derived from that head and public inputs. Raw booking feedback can instead reach the judge or shape a service-visible action. MaskClaw’s strict text and sandbox pass rates are 43 .39% and 16 .93% over 189 calendar candidates (Appendix F, Table 20): gate selectivity, not disclosure or live task completion. SkillLifeBench checks which version actually runs; SkillGuard tests permissions and completion. Neither tests privacy across review, activation, and recipient release [87, 145, 152]. 

7.5 Scenario 3: Withdraw the Fact and Recover State 

Withdraw the fact locally in 𝐻 1 and keep never-exposed 𝐻 0 as the reference, with public tasks and recovery schedule fixed. Reglos tests reader non-use after a tombstone; MemGov certifies constraints on its returned working set. Neither verifies cleanup of other carriers [21, 137]. 

Tracked memory versus new writes. In AfterDeleteBench Table 6 (fixed Qwen weights, 200 IID paired profiles), exact source deletion yields reconstruction, new commitment, and later activation rates of 0 .735 /0.735 /0.510. Adding a sealed literal write gate yields 0 .735 /0/0.680; recorded-lineage closure yields zero observed for all three, as does the clean reference. The gate stops the measured new commitment but leaves other routes to later activation. The closure result transfers as a comparator when lineage is complete in a fixed-weight memory harness, not as evidence that prompts, adapters, or skills were repaired [93]. 22 Zibin Lin, Taotao Wang, and Shengli Zhang 

Why the result does not cover other observers. K-Bench finds that answer-only suppression can leave recovery unchanged through tool observations. SBU tests joint memory and weight suppres-sion on public medical QA, not personal recovery [115, 141]. Probe the restored memory, prompt, weights, and loaded skill against the never-exposed reference. Table 5 and Figure 4 distinguish verification targets; Section 7.7 tests cleanup costs and missing lineage.

7.6 A Linked View Across Booking, Skill Reuse, and Withdrawal 

One observer links three stages by appointment identifier: booking outputs 𝐿 𝑏 (prompt, app transcript, receipt), skill reuse 𝐿 𝑠 (judge exchange, invoked version, service request), and withdrawal 

𝐿 𝑤 (notices, restored outputs, tool exchanges). At a fixed checkpoint, withdrawal in 𝐻 1 and a no-op in 𝐻 0 stay local, including their branch and timing. 

Inherit or account again. Charge 𝐿 𝑏 as in Section 7.2. Reuse is joint post-processing only if it uses accounted outputs and public inputs. A fresh private fit, read outside the existing cap, or release from an unaccounted checkpoint needs its own bound. For a bounded list of new releases with fixed bounds (𝜀 𝑗 , 𝛿 𝑗 ) for the same history pair at every fixed prefix, including accounted intermediates, the joint bound is (𝐾𝜀 0 +𝜀 𝑣 +𝐵 𝑎 + Í𝑗 𝜀 𝑗 , 𝐾𝛿 0 + Í𝑗 𝛿 𝑗 ). Raw-derived judge inputs and fact-dependent notices have no inherited bound; a notice certain in 𝐻 1 and absent in 𝐻 0 forces 𝛿 ≥ 1. 

What withdrawal cannot undo. Local repair cannot recover earlier receipts or judge copies or reset the ledger. A marginal certificate does not cover paired versions, and execution-state replay assumes no committed effects in the affected suffix [14, 44, 138]. This is an analytical trace. 7.7 Diagnostic Experiments: Protection Units and Withdrawal 

These component diagnostics test Table 4, not the full booking trace. Supplementary Section S4 reports protocols and full results. 

Cost of a wider label unit (Stage A).. We fit a logistic head on 24 synthetic profiles and 24 MovieLens 100K users with fixed item and feature slots. At 𝜀 = 16, widening protection from one to 16 labels lowers mean balanced accuracy from 79 .1% to 57 .2% on synthetic profiles and 55 .6% to 50 .2% on MovieLens (clipped non-private baseline: 56 .3%; Figure 6). This does not test item-history privacy or skill execution. 

Deletion scope versus retention. After five target-free writes, source-only deletion yields 1 /24 raw marker hits and 16 /24 confirmed non-target recoveries in A-MEM. Both are 0 /24 after closure deletes all five initial records, versus 0 /24 and 22 /24 for never-exposed rebuild (Figure 5B). In Stage B, closure and equal-count random deletion both erase the initial store (Figure 7). To isolate targeting, compare equal deletion sizes without saturation and test candidate reach separately [144]. 

Incomplete lineage (Stage C).. Across eight single-build states, closure spans 5 /96 records to the full store; write order and target position co-vary. At half trace retention, contiguous gaps miss 79 .0% of recorded descendants versus 36 .9% under random omission (Figure 5A,C). These are reachability losses, not answer leakage.

8.2 Verifiable Withdrawal Across Derived States 

Trace summaries, vectors, skills, and weights, including shared artifacts, through rebuilds and rollbacks. Residue, lineage, and non-use tests distinguish which descendants survive from which affect later decisions [13, 93, 112, 144]. For learned state, specify which observations can influence deletion requests and which version releases the guarantee covers [20, 46]. Empirical probes can expose residue but do not replace the proofs in Table 5. 

8.3 Intra-User Protection Units and Continual Privacy Accounting 

Map each intra-user fact change to the records and tuples it affects. Adaptive composition and continual mean estimation offer budget primitives, but the cohort participation model must be remapped to a single history [39, 124]. Group DP bounds a specified set of edits; inferential privacy instead models alternative secrets and correlated histories, with separate composition conditions [5, 59, 101]. The linked view (Section 7.6) motivates accounting across releases, replay, concurrency, and checkpoints. Renewed consent does not reset past privacy loss. 

8.4 Constrained Evolution of Privacy Mechanisms 

Feedback-driven updates to skills, harnesses, and policies introduce evaluator drift and exposure to external judges [107, 137, 152]. Combine immutable safety invariants and budget gates with versioned contracts, replay, and causal probes of whether the stored policy actually governs behavior [79, 151]. 

8.5 Lifecycle Evaluation and User-Visible Control 

Carry actual state forward through consolidation, withdrawal, recovery, and re-ingestion. Combine answer, tool, and skill probes with tests of recipient appropriateness, cross-carrier reacquisition, and retained utility [38, 70, 82, 96, 114, 128, 129, 132]. Donated memories inform sensitive-field selection [25]; vary linking cues and action-based exposure [66, 136]. Record consent, service-visible actions, completion, and refusal at each checkpoint [41, 43, 73]. Finally, user control requires an inspectable state machine letting users query derived artifacts, track withdrawal propagation, and inspect shared-artifact or retention bounds (Table 6). 26 Zibin Lin, Taotao Wang, and Shengli Zhang 

Table 6. Core Research Gaps, Minimum Mechanism Requirements, and Minimum Verification Evidence    

> Research Gap Minimum Mechanism Requirements Minimum Verification Evidence
> Continual intra-user privacy (Section 4, Table 4) Define fact/attribute/window adjacency, shared sources, and joint transcript. Report multi-round composition, exhaustion fallback, and replay accounting. Derived-state withdrawal (Section 5.3, Table 5) Maintain provenance graphs, dependency closures, shared policies, and weight sync. Test write–query–delete–probe– recovery–re-ingestion across carriers. Certified deletion (Table 5) Separate logical invalidation, physical erasure, and formal non-reconstructability. Specify adversary access, certificate semantics, and post-backup/rollback checks. Privacy-mechanism evolution (Section 6) Enforce immutable invariants, budget gates, regression suites, and rollback admission. Report pre-/post-mutation surfaces, invariants, and causal failure cases. Personal-device deployment (Section 7.6) Decompose local compute, cloud network, and crash-recovery overhead. Report per-update cost, memory footprint, traffic, latency, and energy draw.

9 Limitations of the Review 

The evidence distribution describes the screened corpus through 10 November 2026. The search windows provide different coverage across carriers and observer interfaces; zero direct studies in a carrier cell does not establish absence in the literature. The historical Stage 2 exclusion trail is incomplete. Agreement counts cover selected recoded cases, not the full corpus. Direct observations come from controlled memory workflows, with source-specific assumptions about state carryover, tracing, and the measured outcome. The source studies span formal analyses, fixed benchmarks, simulated histories, and prototypes. HomeLLaMA adds a controlled study with real participants and an architectural bound on direct profile transmission. Section 7 compares worked scenarios, not an integrated implementation. The diagnostic experiments use fixed-slot heads, synthetic memory histories, or offline graphs; they do not estimate end-to-end privacy or utility after deployment. 

10 Conclusion 

To transfer a privacy result, fix the history change, trace its derived artifacts, and account for the observer’s full view. The booking case exposes lost action options; the feedback case separates reuse of an accounted head from raw-data distillation; withdrawal separates reader non-use from cleanup and prior disclosure. The next test should measure these trade-offs on one linked transcript spanning a fresh update, skill reuse, and withdrawal. 

Data and Reproducibility Availability 

The Supplementary Material PDF (Sections S1–S6) gives the search, coding, diagnostic, and com-parison protocols. The anonymized archive includes search and screening ledgers through Stage 15, frozen diagnostic results, and CPU-runnable integrity checks. Supplementary Section S4.5 specifies the separate setup needed for a model rerun. 

Ethics Statement 

This review synthesizes publicly available scholarly literature and runs bounded diagnostics on synthetic markers and public MovieLens 100K data without redistributing raw ratings. No human participants, user studies, or animal experiments were conducted, and no new personally identifiable Privacy Protection in Self-Evolving Personal Agents 27 

information was collected. Ethics exemption applicability will be confirmed under venue policies prior to publication.
