TAOTAO WANG, Shenzhen University, China 

SHENGLI ZHANG, Shenzhen University, China This supplement documents searches and candidate decisions for literature available through 10 November 2026. It includes a deduplicated source ledger, primary-source locators for 22 examples, and diagnostic protocols and results. Sections S5 and S6 give the operation matrix and source-level checks for the three deployment comparisons. CCS Concepts: • Security and privacy → Privacy protections ; • Computing methodologies → Intelli-gent agents ; Continual learning ; • Information systems → Personalization .Additional Key Words and Phrases: personal agents, self-evolution, privacy protection, continual learning, long-term memory, machine unlearning 

S1 Literature Search Protocol, Version-Pinning Policy, and Screening Records S1.1 Review Scope, Cutoff Date, and Multi-Stage Search Architecture 

The review uses a bounded thematic search for privacy mechanisms, their prerequisites, and evidence about their interfaces. The literature cutoff is 10 November 2026 . Each retained work had a publicly accessible primary manuscript or authoritative archival record by that date. The initial search ran on 10–11 November and the revision checks on 18–19 November. To ensure sufficient depth across both direct personal-agent prototypes and transferable formal primitives without conflating general agent capabilities with privacy guarantees, the original 75-source corpus was assembled in three stages and then expanded: (1) Stage 1: Core Direct-Intersection Baseline and Bounded Gap Audit (Sources #1– #25; executed 10–11 November 2026). Starting from an initial seed analysis of update-operator mechanisms, we executed a structured gap audit targeting four sparse areas of the mechanism map: (i) the direct intersection of personal agents, self-evolution, and privacy; (ii) persistent memory forgetting and derived-state deletion; (iii) user-level, continual, and cross-round formal guarantees; and (iv) private feedback, preference, experience, and skill updates. This stage established the 25-source core baseline comprising 20 representative mechanisms (including newly identified SBU [133] coded as T and Galaxy [6] coded as S), four adjacent safety and privacy surveys [12, 13, 34, 71], and one operational definition survey on self-evolving agents [43]. (2) Stage 2: Operator-Adjacent Thematic Expansion (Sources #26–#62; +37 unique sources; executed 11 November 2026). While holding the long-term single-user personal-agent scope fixed, we expanded retrieval across seven adjacent mechanism, primitive, and evaluation themes: prompt and parameter differential privacy (DP), preference protection, continual learning and adaptive composition, private retrieval and oblivious dynamic indexing, machine unlearning and deletion verification, information-flow control and skill evolution, and lifecycle attack and residue benchmarks. All 25 core sources were retained and 37 non-duplicate sources were added, yielding 62 unique sources.  

> Authors’ Contact Information: Zibin Lin, Shenzhen University, Shenzhen, China, linaacc9595@gmail.com; Taotao Wang, Shenzhen University, Shenzhen, China, ttwang@szu.edu.cn; Shengli Zhang, Shenzhen University, Shenzhen, China, zsl@ szu.edu.cn. 2Zibin Lin, Taotao Wang, and Shengli Zhang

(3) Stage 3: Targeted ACM Venue Supplementation (Sources #63–#75; +13 unique sources; executed 11 November 2026). To address under-representation of ACM archival venues in the 62-source corpus, we conducted a targeted search over the 10.1145 DOI prefix (publication window 2018-01-01 to 2026-11-10 ) across four focal areas: preference learning in personal privacy assistants, agent context and tool information flow, machine unlearning verification and version-differential leakage, and backward-secure dynamic searchable encryption. This stage added 13 peer-reviewed ACM sources (bringing the initial corpus to 75 unique sources , of which 22 representative mechanisms are compared at operator level in Main Table 3) and documented six substantively related excluded ACM candidates. (4) Stage 4: Targeted coverage and boundary check (Sources #76–#86; +11 net; 18 Novem-ber 2026). We held the literature cutoff fixed and screened the works listed in Section S1.6. Ten publications were newly added and AUBench was moved from a contextual citation into the evidence corpus. This stage brought the corpus to 86 sources. (5) Stage 5: Cross-session relation follow-up (Source #87; +1; 18 November 2026). An exact-title and arXiv identifier check added MemJoin as a risk and evaluation source with a scoped read-intervention code. This stage brought the corpus to 87 sources. (6) Stage 6: Core-coverage recheck (Sources #88–#93; +6; 18 November 2026). S1.2 Queried Information Sources, Access Boundaries, and Stopping Criteria 

Candidate discovery and primary-source verification combined scholarly indexes, DOI metadata registries, open-access preprint APIs, and official conference/journal proceedings. Crucially, anti-bot gates, human-verification challenges, and session timeouts encountered during interactive web queries were recorded strictly as incomplete channel access rather than zero hits or negative domain evidence. In IEEE Xplore (queried 10 November 2026), a strict three-way intersection over personal agents, privacy, and continual/self-evolving updates returned 0 hits. A broader query ( personalized AND agent AND privacy ) returned 335 records; however, narrowing those records with self-evolving ,

memory , or continual learning yielded no candidates with a localizable privacy mechanism intervening in persistent state updates. These counts apply strictly to the recorded query syntax and date rather than the entire IEEE Xplore database. In the ACM Digital Library (queried 10–11 November 2026), submitting the interactive advanced-search query "personal agent" AND privacy AND ("self-evolving" OR "continual learning" OR memory) triggered a Cloudflare verification block, and subse-quent automated browser retries timed out without returning sequential result pages. Rather than recording zero hits, Stage 3 pivoted to Crossref metadata queries scoped to prefix:10.1145 

and exact-title lookups, followed by manual verification against official ACM DOI pages or author-archived camera-ready PDFs. Similarly, Google Scholar exact-title and forward-citation queries (10 November 2026) triggered human-verification challenges; this channel was logged as incomplete and neither interpreted as zero citations nor used to satisfy stopping criteria. Table S1 summarizes all queried information sources and their interpretive boundaries. 

Two-Tier Stopping Rule and Methodological Boundaries. Search termination followed a two-tier protocol. At the direct-intersection tier (Stage 1), retrieval halted once all newly surfaced candidates were verified against primary texts and two consecutive query families plus accessible one-hop exact-title/related-paper checks yielded no new personal-agent mechanism category (producing only duplicates, evaluation benchmarks, or established exclusion categories). In Stage 1, after arXiv queries A1 and A2 surfaced Galaxy [6] and SBU [133], queries A3 –A13 and accessible Q5 checks returned only previously captured methods, background benchmarks, or excluded items. At the 

thematic expansion tier (Stages 2–3), retrieval halted once all seven operator themes possessed mechanism, boundary, and risk/evaluation evidence and successive candidates fell into duplicate versions, identical technical precedents, or established exclusion codes. Because interactive ACM DL result pagination and Google Scholar forward-citation tracking were obstructed, and citation chasing was bounded to one hop, this protocol supports reproducible mechanism comparison within the initial 75-source corpus without claiming field-wide exhaustiveness; absence of evidence in our bounded search is never restated as proof of non-existence across the literature.

S1.4 Inclusion Criteria, Exclusion Codes, and Version-Pinning Policy 

Mechanism Eligibility and Orthogonal Coding Rules. A study had to satisfy six criteria for mecha-nism eligibility. Table S10 gives the current mechanism and background counts. (1) Cutoff and primary availability: An original research paper or authoritative archival record was publicly accessible on or before 10 November 2026. (2) Persistent state or protected state interface: The system updates persistent state across parameters, prompts/control policies, memories, or skills/experiences, constrains read/context release over such state, or provides a formal composition, selection, indexing, or unlearning-verification primitive directly mappable to those operators. (3) Localizable operator intervention: The primary text identifies the specific candidate-generation, scoring, writeback, maintenance, read-release, withdrawal/deletion, or skill-admission step where protection or auditing intervenes. (4) Explicit threat/interface specification: At least two of the following three elements are confirmable from the primary text: protection unit (protected object) , observer , and trusted endpoint / trust assumption .(5) Verifiable support: The protection claim has a reported evaluation or an analytical result that can be located in the primary paper. Design descriptions and worked examples alone supply background. (6) Operational evidence-distance assignment: Evidence distance ( D, S, T) is coded by proximity to a long-term single-user personal-agent update loop—never inferred merely from surface labels such as private , local , on-device , federated , or self-evolving —and is recorded orthogonally to guarantee type (DP, cryptographic/system confidentiality, or empirical governance/behavioral gating). Sources without a credited protection operation establish selected definitions, interface threat surfaces, design context, and evaluation targets. A study with both risk measurements and a specified and evaluated defense can enter the risk stratum and contribute a mechanism code for that defense. When the primary text locates a privacy control and specifies its interface, an evaluation can qualify the operation for coding. We code the guarantee independently and credit a stated DP budget only to the extent that the source supports the corresponding adjacency, accounting, and observable release. Table S3 gives the screening codes. 

Deduplication and Canonical Version-Pinning Policy. Preprints, OpenReview submissions, non-archival workshop papers, and peer-reviewed conference or journal articles representing the same method family were deduplicated into a single corpus entry. Version selection followed three deterministic rules: 

• Archival precedence: Whenever a peer-reviewed conference or journal version was verifiable on or before 10 November 2026 (via ACL Anthology , ACM Digital Library ,

IEEE Xplore , PMLR , NeurIPS , ICLR , COLM , TMLR , USENIX , or AAAI ), the archival publication 8 Zibin Lin, Taotao Wang, and Shengli Zhang                

> Table S3. Standardized Mechanism-Screening Exclusion and Disposition Codes ( E1 –E9 )
> Code Exclusion or Non-Mechanism Disposition Criterion
> E1 Static personalization or single-turn inference without a protected persistent-state interface or a specified primitive that transfers to one.
> E2 Neither updates a persistent state carrier nor constrains read/context release over a persistent state.
> E3 No identifiable privacy intervention at an operation; a local ,on-device ,federated ,safe , or
> privacy-first label cannot establish the operation.
> E4 Attack, leakage/risk analysis, survey, or evaluation benchmark only, without intervening in a protection workflow (eligible only as non-mechanism background/risk context unless also contributing a transferable mitigation or private release mechanism, as in [16, 128]).
> E5 Secondary commentary, blog post, or promotional page that cannot be verified against a primary research manuscript.
> E6 Duplicate version, mirror, or renamed release within a method family. Earlier ledgers also used this code for operation overlap. The follow-up retains independent studies that contribute new comparisons or endpoints.
> E7 Non-personal-agent domain whose mechanism lies outside the target state-update and release chain.
> E8 Restricted full text or insufficient accessible technical evidence as of the cutoff date.
> E9 First public release occurred after the 10 November 2026 literature cutoff date.

was adopted as the canonical citation (e.g., STOC 2019 for [84], PACMMOD/PODS 2026 for [56], and TMLR formal editions for [43, 125, 128]). For journals whose online-first date precedes volume/issue assignment, the bibliography records the official volume publication year while noting the online-first year in verification logs (specifically PANOLA [131], ACM TOIT Vol. 22, 2022, first online 2021; and Machine Unlearning: A Survey [154], ACM CSUR Vol. 56, 2024, first online 2023). 

• Explicit versioned preprint pinning: Works without formal archival pagination by 10 November 2026 were pinned to an exact versioned preprint ( arXiv:...vN with its DataCite record, or TechRxiv v1 Crossref DOI for [13]), using the submission year of the cited version. In particular, PrivDPO [61] was verified on the official ACM CCS 2026 accepted-papers list, but because proceedings pagination was not yet registered at the cutoff date, it is pinned to 

arXiv:2608.05040v1 . Likewise, Deployment-Time Memorization [15] appeared at the non-archival ICML 2026 MemFM Workshop and is pinned to arXiv:2606.10062v2 (with author attribution verified directly against the versioned PDF). 

• One-to-one citation integrity: Every corpus source appears in the supplementary bibli-ography and maps to one BibTeX key across the submission.

S1.6 Targeted Revision Search and Candidate Decisions 

On 18 November 2026 we ran focused web searches for evidence that could change the protected unit, the comparison between writing and release, or the interpretation of withdrawal. The literature cutoff remained 10 November 2026. The search strings included persistent memory personal agent privacy write-time response intervention , persistent memory agent after deletion benchmark provenance closure preservation , and longitudinal privacy secret pairs Pufferfish Markov Quilt composition adaptive machine unlearning deletion privacy , with exact-title and identifier follow-up on arXiv, publisher, and proceedings records. The following log records the candidates taken to primary-source review for this check. Ranked search returns do not provide stable channel-wide denominators. #88, CIMemories [93]. Include as risk and evaluation (ICLR 2026, Sections 4–5, especially the violation/completeness, repeated-task, and privacy-prompting evaluations). The synthetic memory is evaluated across recipient–task contexts and runs. Generic privacy instructions serve as comparison conditions, and the outcomes reveal a violation–completeness trade-off. No mechanism code ( E4 ). 

#89, HUSH-Bench [156]. Include as risk and evaluation (arXiv v2, revised 3 August 2026, Sections 3.3–3.4 and 4.4–4.5, Tables 4–5). The benchmark isolates exact-target exposure in retrieved input, unsolicited use of sensitive history in the answer, and use after explicit invitation. Retrieval interfaces and invitation are diagnostic comparison conditions. No mechanism code ( E4 ). 

#90, Memora [130]. Include as risk and evaluation (Findings of ACL 2026, benchmark con-struction and Forgetting-Aware Memory Accuracy). The benchmark penalizes use of su-perseded memories in longitudinal tasks. It supplies an obsolete-state reuse check for withdrawal evaluation. No mechanism code ( E4 ). 

#91, AIM [63]. Include as an extended mechanism, T (arXiv v1, 11 September 2026, Sec-tions 3.4, 3.7, and 5). Pre-storage visibility classification and index filtering restrict which other users can retrieve private entries. Classification and enforcement depend on the trusted platform. The multi-user prototype tests access scope rather than inference about an attribute within one user’s history. 

#92, PAHF [78]. Include as state background (arXiv v1, 18 February 2026, Sections 3.2 and 4.2). Pre-action clarification and post-action correction show how a natural-language prefer-ence can enter persistent memory and shape subsequent actions. The evaluation targets adaptation to preference drift. No mechanism code ( E4 ). 

#93, PeCL [168]. Include as an extended mechanism, T (EMNLP 2026, Sections 3.2–3.3 and Appendix G). It perturbs clipped token embeddings and uses sensitivity-guided retention during task-incremental training. Its appendix calibrates a Gaussian step for a specified noise scale. A joint adaptation-and-release guarantee would need to control the data-dependent scale, neighboring contextual features, retention decisions, and repeated access. Code T

credits the local intervention and task-level evaluation. 

Recode #59, AntiSkillBench [150]. Keep the primary risk role and add T for its active pre-distillation sanitization (arXiv v1, Sections 4.4 and 5.3, Table 2 and Appendix C.1). The operation transforms persona dialogue before a distiller sees it. The study tests scripted 16 Zibin Lin, Taotao Wang, and Shengli Zhang 

trace corpora and fixed distillation runs. Its provenance signals address unauthorized reuse detection as a separate claim. 

Duplicates. The CIMemories preprint (2511.14937) and Memora preprint (2604.20006) collapse into their archival records ( E6 ). SP-Mem, DP-ES, AUBench, and MaskClaw already have corpus rows.

#101. Profile-DP, arXiv v1 (19 October 2026), extended T. Theorem 1, Section 4.4, and Ap-pendix A give a local release bound for withheld values of a fixed personal profile while authorized attributes and the sharing context are public. Reusing a privatized profile and regenerating it have different accounting. The inference experiments hold the source profile fixed [76]. 

#102. UMPeek, arXiv v1 (3 September 2026), risk/evaluation with S for the stateful coun-terfactual release gate. Section VII and Tables IV–V evaluate disclosure and utility under repeated requests in four personalized benchmark settings. The external observer sees ordinary choices and responses. A trusted evaluator compares personalized and unper-sonalized behavior before release. The real-system study in Section VI covers the threat assessment [75]. 20 Zibin Lin, Taotao Wang, and Shengli Zhang 

#103. Online and offline KL-regularized RLHF, arXiv v1 (15 October 2025), extended T. Sec-tion 5 and Algorithm 2 localize randomized response to user-side preference labels in the online contextual-bandit model. The policy learns from reported labels, while the theorem’s privacy unit is the label. Section 6 tests the offline algorithm. The NeurIPS ResponsibleFM workshop version belongs to the same family [149]. 

#104. From One Query to Many , arXiv v2 (23 October 2026), survey/background. Sections 4–5 compare protection granularity, accounting scope, and observable releases in RAG. This is a baseline for the read-time analysis and for distinguishing the cross-carrier contribution [175]. 

#105. He et al., ACM Computing Surveys 58(6), article 162 (published online 9 December 2025, April 2026 issue), survey/background. Sections 3–5 organize agent threats and defenses, including attacker inference and workflow exposure [55]. 

#106. Beyond Centralization , archival UMAP 2026 demo paper, extended T. Sections 2.1 and 3 identify client-side clipping and Gaussian perturbation on model updates derived from viewing histories before sharing with an aggregator. The 53-day, 22-participant deployment measures recommendation use and user control. The paper reports a nominal privacy parameter without a complete adjacency, failure probability, or repeated-release account-ing contract. We record the deployment and intervention, and leave its DP guarantee unclassified [117]. 

#107. MemGate, arXiv v1 (4 June 2026), extended S. Sections IV–V and Figure 3 evaluate a query-conditioned gate on retrieved personal memory before generation. The gate receives candidate memories and reduces measured cross-domain failures in benchmark tests using memory frameworks and the OpenClaw runtime. The tests use scripted tasks [172]. 

#108. Revoked but Still Authoritative , arXiv v1 (8 September 2026), risk/evaluation with T.Section 4.2 and Appendix C.1 (Table 14) locate a read-time validity guard and test later decisions when operating on retained policy records. The store-level filter prevents the measured unsafe action in the single-read condition. Derived journal records can still influ-ence decisions after write-back. The target is non-use of a revoked policy in organizational scenarios [112]. 

#109. What to Admit and How to Present , arXiv v1 (8 October 2026), extended S. Sections 3.2 and 4.1 and Sections 5.1–5.5 distinguish the admission of recalled personal information into the generator’s context from rendering an unchanged admitted set. The adjudicator sees the retrieved candidates. The evaluation uses fixed memory pools and a personal-memory benchmark substrate and measures task-specific failure and beneficial personalization [82]. 

#110. Audience-Bound Persistent Memory, arXiv v1 (28 September 2026), extended D for write and derivation controls and S for per-attempt context admission. Sections 3.1–3.5, Section 6.4, and Appendix E specify audience binding at ingest, restrictions on derived audiences, and mediated admission into model calls. The longitudinal unit is one owner across synthetic multi-party histories. The native checks cover a scoped validation frame. The formal guarantee concerns assembled context under reliable identity, provenance, and complete mediation [85]. The additions contributed one D,three S, and four T source rollups. The audience-bound study adds evidence about memory writing and derivation, and its retrieval result is recorded independently. sortOrder=descending . The parameter query required a second page starting at 100. We retrieved every hit returned for each expression. The arXiv date criterion applies to first submission, and the returned versions used for this round were all available by the cutoff. We used archived versions when available. For the archival check, we ran five title and abstract filters over the official ACL Anthology BibTeX export with abstracts (build dated 17 November 2026). The eligible window was 24,844 28 Zibin Lin, Taotao Wang, and Shengli Zhang 

main-conference and Findings records from 2024 through the cutoff. The archive contains the venue patterns, exact regular expressions, deterministic ID ordering, and the export’s SHA-256. P and W query prompt and adaptation terms with a privacy term in the title and a language-agent or personalization term in the title or abstract. R requires a preference, alignment, reward or recommendation title and differential-privacy wording. M requires a memory or unlearning title and a privacy or lifecycle-governance term. K requires a skill title and a privacy, permission, or provenance term. Stable identifiers, abstracts, and primary-source checks support the candidate dispositions in the archived ledger.

Screening and linkage. We considered title and abstract for each record, checked duplicates against the starting corpus and within the round, and inspected primary texts for new inclusions. Previously represented works retained their indices and canonical citations. Transfer comparators contribute when they establish an additional observer, unit, operator, or directly useful comparative result. Static selection or architecture alone can enter as design context. An evaluated study of current-query rewriting, for example, can supply a transferable control without counting as evidence for release from retained personal memory. The candidate files record exclusions under the existing E codes, reasons, and original version or anthology identifiers. Existing and newly accepted works link to source indices or canonical proceedings IDs. The 21 arXiv additions and five ACL additions represent distinct new works after cross-channel matching. 

Archival checks. MemGov, flagged in the review and recovered by the M query, supplies surface evidence for scoped context assembly [23]. COLLABMEM, PRISM, and SkillLadder add transfer evidence for team memory, client-private tuning, and skill sharing, respectively. TimlMemBench supplies risk and evaluation evidence [17, 142, 148, 179]. A separate exact-title check in the NeurIPS proceedings adds Hanke et al.’s matched prompt and parameter adaptation comparisons [54]. The exact-title result is additional to the table’s channel denominators. 

Coding sensitivity. A separate AI recoder, given the rubric and primary source identifiers before seeing the new decisions, reviewed nine selected arXiv cases spanning prompt tuning, personal adapters, memory release, and preference learning (P10, P13, W9, W65, W79, W116, R19, R25, R28). Eight of nine initial distance calls agreed, counting the absence of a mechanism code as a distinct outcome. The difference concerned R25. The second reading classified its current-query rewrite under a fixed privacy profile as T; the initial reading treated the fixed rule as sufficient for S. We retained T because the test protects current-query content while applying a fixed rule, and does not exercise private facts read from retained memory [103]. The alternative would move one source and one operation from T to S, leaving the direct-evidence finding intact. Supplementary Material: Privacy Protection in Self-Evolving Personal Agents 29

The added operations comprise two surface and 23 transfer rows. The 11 direct rows from nine controlled memory sources remain the direct evidence in the screened corpus. The recorded lexical and venue windows define the coverage of this audit. The anonymized archive’s literature_screening/ directory contains replay scripts, exact arXiv request URLs and page counts, and both complete per-candidate TSV files. Those ledgers preserve the observed hits when the public index versions change. 

S1.15 Observer and Withdrawal Follow-up 

On 19 November 2026 we ran a targeted follow-up with the same 10 November cutoff. We searched public scholarly and proceedings pages for combinations of language agents, joint or multi-channel observation, tool-result inference, unlearning, derived state, and activation after deletion. We traced the withdrawal references of Lin et al.’s expanded memory-governance survey (v5, §6 and Appendix D) and checked proceedings and version histories against primary texts [80]. This was a targeted search for comparisons that could change the interpretation of Main Section 7. The archive records the query strings and targeted decisions. The shortlist is defined by those logged checks. 

Known-source recovery check. We matched identifiers, DOIs, and normalized titles for the nine known direct sources in Table S9 against the complete Stage 13 arXiv and anthology ledgers. Five of nine were recovered (MemPrivacy, Opal, SP-Mem, Disclosure Begets Disclosure, and HomeLLaMA). HealthJev, Reglos, AfterDeleteBench, and Audience-Bound Persistent Memory appear in the corpus through earlier stages and had no match in either final carrier window. HomeLLaMA matched two arXiv queries and counts once. This check tests recovery of a known positive set, with each study counted once. Differences in retrieval coverage limit interpretation of zero cells across carriers. 

Joint observation and withdrawal. K-Bench (source #166, v1) follows a referral from Lin et al. Its single-source injection test compares weight, prompt, and retrieval interventions using six observation channels. Its tool-path evidence changes how we interpret reduced answer disclosure after withdrawal [166]. TOP-Bench (source #167, EMNLP Findings) tests combined inference over tool returns and evaluates response mitigations in fixed tasks. The archive links its preprint to the proceedings record. The proceedings article was absent from the Stage 13 candidate hits even though that venue was in the archival window [101]. CIPL (source #168, arXiv:2609.21686v1) evaluates recoverable leakage with channel-specific observations and compares audited and unaudited paths. The earlier public CIPL manuscript (arXiv:2603.22751v2) shares its evaluation framework and overlapping experimental cases. We retain the later expanded report as one source for this comparison [59]. We also rechecked the matched control and separate reconstruction, commitment, and activation outcomes in AfterDeleteBench. We recoded MemCut (existing source #43) to credit its fixed-budget cleanup comparison as T/B. It measures recovery after the intervention at a fixed checkpoint. The direct code for withdrawal requires carryover to later decisions or interactions [106, 170]. 

Independent evidence for the same operation. Stage 13 candidate W50 was excluded on operation overlap. Its full text (source #169) compares DP-SGD, LoRA, and full fine-tuning on common tasks, with separate empirical memorization, utility, and compute measures. That comparison is eligible even though the operators already occur in the corpus. We code the benchmark as transferable evidence for parameter updates [26]. 30 Zibin Lin, Taotao Wang, and Shengli Zhang 

The follow-up adds four risk and evaluation sources and credits the existing MemCut comparator. At the end of Stage 14, the corpus had 169 sources and 173 credited operation rows. Closest-source totals for the 133 studies with a credited intervention were nine direct, 14 surface, and 110 transferable. The direct rows are unchanged by these decisions. They describe the screened corpus. 

S1.16 Complete Observer and Withdrawal Windows and E6 Rescreening 

On 19 November 2026 we searched two bibliographic windows under the unchanged cutoff. The joint-observation query combines agent, privacy and terms for internal channels or tool returns. The withdrawal query combines agent and memory with terms for deletion, derived state or selective forgetting. We reran these title and abstract filters on the same 24,844-record ACL Anthology snapshot as Stage 13. We screened the one joint and three withdrawal matches in canonical identifier order. In Crossref we screened the first 12 results in API relevance order for each query using the 2024–2026 publication window through 10 November. The anonymized archive’s protocol and ledgers record the exact expressions, date boundaries, source versions and reasons for all 28 candidate occurrences. The arXiv Atom search was rate-limited or timed out. The two completed search channels and the separate exact checks supply the screening evidence. 

Linked-observer records. The ACL window recovers TOP-Bench [101]. An exact DOI check adds AgentLeak as a risk and evaluation source with a transferable release result [36]. The paper compares final-output and internal-channel controls in scripted multi-agent tasks (Sections VI-E/F and VII-C). In its main traces, an apparently clean final answer can co-occur with internal exposure. The observed channels and tested controls inform the linked-recipient comparison in Main Section 7.3. The withdrawal window recovers TimlMemBench [179]. The cutoff and targeted windows continue to limit generalization from the direct-evidence counts. 

Reconsideration of operation-overlap exclusions. We obtained all 40 versioned primary abstracts for Stage 13 arXiv rows previously excluded under E6. We reassessed them for a distinct observer interface or an independent matched privacy, utility or resource result. Full-text verification retained ten additional works, including matched memorization and resource comparisons, post-training suppression under equal pruning budgets, and studies that vary what training recipients can see. W50 was already restored in Stage 14. The remaining 29 decisions are recorded individually. Supplementary Section S6.4 gives the comparisons and evidence boundaries. The corpus now contains 180 studies. The operation matrix contains 184 credited rows from 144 studies, with closest-source counts of nine direct, 14 surface and 121 transferable. The Stage 13 known-source recovery result remains five of nine. That count describes the original search windows.

S3 Operator-Level Verification Ledger for the 22 Representative Mechanisms 

This section provides the detailed primary-source verification ledger for the 22 representative privacy mechanisms synthesized in the main text. The codes in Table S11 identify the focal operation for each exemplar. Section S5 separates additional operations within those exemplars and applies the same coding across the mechanism-eligible corpus. Two orthogonal dimensions are recorded throughout: Supplementary Material: Privacy Protection in Self-Evolving Personal Agents 45

> Surveys & state background 00019 19 Mechanism-Eligibility / Corpus Column Total 914 121 36 180

(1) Evidence distance ( D/S/T). D denotes a protected update to durable single-user state carried across decisions or interactions. S denotes a tested read or release of retained state in a personal-agent task. T denotes results transferable from another setting. Each code refers to the focal operation. (2) Guarantee class: Differential privacy (DP), cryptographic or hardware-enforced confi-dentiality (MPC, TEE/ORAM), and empirical governance or behavioral gating are tracked separately so that scenario proximity ( D/S/T) is never conflated with formal guarantee strength. Table S11 records, for each of the 22 representative mechanisms, its state carrier and coding, exact protection unit, private-input access boundary and trusted endpoints, constrained observable interface together with multi-round and withdrawal conditions, and verified primary-source loca-tors (sections, definitions, theorems, propositions, algorithms, figures, and tables in the archival publication or pinned preprint). Where an archival paper or ACL/Nature-style manuscript uses unnumbered top-level headings (e.g., [69, 74]), locators cite the exact formal statements, named headings, figures, tables, and numbered appendices rather than imputed section numbers. 

Representative selection and boundary checks. For prompt and parameter updates, DP-OPT, DP-ES, Private Selection, and user-level DP fine-tuning distinguish candidate generation, private scoring, selection over private candidates, and contribution clipping. PromptDPSGD and zeroth-order tuning remain in the extended set as alternative implementation routes. PrivMeSA provides an empirical cohort-trained risk-reward contrast. Decoupled reward modeling, DP Reward Estimation, and KL-Regularization DP distinguish tuple protection, label protection, and single-output sampling. PrivDPO and sparse reward estimation extend the label case. SecP-Tuning represents confidential execution, and Lifelong DP and Concurrent Composition separate a version-release protocol from composition across mechanisms. For memory, MemPrivacy and HealthJev contrast representation substitution with writeback decisions, and SP-Mem is discussed as a variant. Opal represents dynamic confidential maintenance. Oblix and Compass provide alternative indexing trust models, and Compass supplies batched ORAM proof results used by Opal [67, 94, 181]. DP-MemView, Galaxy, PANOLA, and AirGapAgent distinguish attribute-limited reading, cloud masking, learned disclosure policies, and disclosure to a third party. The uncertainty-aware assistant extends the learned policy case. Reglos and SBU distinguish invalidation in a personal workflow from joint parameter and memory cleanup in a 46 Zibin Lin, Taotao Wang, and Shengli Zhang 

public-task evaluation. MaskClaw and FedSkillGit distinguish scenario-level skill filtering from federated publication. FederatedSkill extends the latter. These groupings explain the main table choices and leave the other studies available in Table S9 and the relevant sections. Section S3.1 records the earlier boundary recheck and the Stage 4 decisions. AUBench [79] contributes to risk and evaluation counts. Xu et al. [157] remains a contextual graph-model reference outside the corpus.

Preference output 

Code: T

(Single-action label-DP )Main theorem: single preference label (contexts/actions public; appendix also analyzes full-tuple add/remove). Soft prompt 

Code: T

(2PC/MPC confidentiality )Cryptographic confidentiality of input tokens and soft-prompt shares (no DP adjacency unit). Two semi-honest, non-colluding online servers execute secret-shared forward passes; a trusted third-party dealer 𝑇 

supplies offline correlated randomness (collusion of 𝑇 with either server breaks online input secrecy); data owner locally holds labels, plaintext loss, and CMA-ES prompt updates. Hides inputs and intermediate activations from servers during forward-only tuning relative to an approximated fixed-point Random Feature Attention (ΠRFA ) circuit rather than exact Softmax attention; reconstructs up to 8 ,000 plaintext forward logit outputs to the data owner during CMA-ES tuning. Does not bound statistical leakage from allowed outputs/answers; composes per protocol execution under non-collusion; no withdrawal operator. §4.1–§4.3; §5 Tables 1–3; App. §6.4–§6.5 (Algs. 1–2, Security Analysis, Table 5).

Lifelong DP [72] 

Continual parameters 

Code: T

(Tuple-level Lifelong DP )Tuple-level neighboring task and episodic-memory datasets (Def. 2). The protocol uses fixed, disjoint task datasets or batches for episodic reuse. Trusted central learner and episodic-memory manager access raw task streams and enforce strict partition non-overlap and prescribed memory reuse. Objective/gradient perturbation releases a sequence of model checkpoints under a constant tuple-level pure 

𝜀 -Lifelong-DP ( 𝛿 = 0, Thm. 2) bound against observers of all versions. Correlated tuples remain within the guarantee when the same tuple adjacency and protocol apply. Multi-tuple fact changes and extra observation paths require separate analysis. §3 (Defs. 2–3, Alg. 1); §4 (Thm. 2); §5 (Alg. 2); §6. Concurrent Composition [56] 

Multi-mechanism composition 

Code: T

(DP composition framework )Event-level or user-level stream adjacency specified via an explicit verification function. Meta-framework over stateful continual DP mechanisms; requires each component mechanism to satisfy DP independently under declared verification semantics. Bounds privacy of the full interactive transcript under adaptive interleaving of dataset updates and queries. Proves naive parallel composition fails for approximate (𝜀, 𝛿 )-DP (𝛿 > 0) under adaptive updates while giving tight replacement bounds and privacy filters (and recovering parallel composition for pure DP, Cor. 5.5); does not privatize non-DP modules. §3 (Def. 3.7); §4 (Thm. 4.12); §5 (Thms. 5.2, 5.4, Cor. 5.5); §7 (Thm. 7.6); §8. MemPrivacy [18] 

Memory (writeback) 

Code: D

(Conditional system isolation )Policy-classified sensitive spans (PL2–PL4 raw values); non-DP. Trusted edge device runs an offline-trained span detector, replaces sensitive spans with typed placeholders, stores persistent value–placeholder mappings locally, and restores values locally after cloud generation. Cloud memory services (LangMem, Mem0, Memobase) store and retrieve only placeholder-sanitized text across sessions. Unrecognized spans, allowed typed structure, and cross-placeholder inferences remain visible to cloud; no formal DP bound or downstream derived-state deletion. §3; §4.1 (Alg. 1); §4.4; §5.1–§5.3 (Tables 2–4); App. D.3–D.4 (Tables 6–7).

PANOLA [131] 

Privacy policy 

Code: S

(Empirical release governance )Co-owned OSN content items subject to multi-party sharing conflicts (non-DP). Context release 

Code: S

(Empirical context minimization )Task-irrelevant personal profile fields under contextual-integrity norms (non-DP). Restricts execution agent’s context window to minimizer-approved fields and escalates scope shifts to explicit user confirmation (97% protection vs. 45% unmitigated baseline under single-query context hijacking). Protects session release over static synthetic profiles without persistent self-evolving minimizer updates, formal multi-round bounds, or state unlearning. §3 (Contextual Integrity threat model); §4 (Minimizer architecture, Fig. 1); §5 (Fig. 2, Table 1). 

S3.1 Targeted Recheck of Screening and Evidence-Distance Decisions 

Scope and record. On 18 November 2026 we rechecked seven records from Table S9 and one excluded candidate from Table S4. We selected cases that could plausibly change category if a reader coded the entire architecture, treated any long-running loop as personal, or conflated the paper’s primary role with its eligibility. We used the archival PANOLA text, the revision-pinned versions indicated below, and the full-text revision of the Tobaben et al. journal paper. Each entry gives the decision in the frozen ledger or screening log, a plausible competing reading, the primary-source locator, and the ruling. The existing classifications were visible during this single-pass recheck, which used AI assistance to locate and read the cited source passages. Competing readings are decision checks. They are not votes from separate coders. The earlier FedSkillGit classification in BR6 is an exception in that the draft history records an actual change of code. 

Adjudication rule. We first checked whether the paper specifies its own protection step, distin-guishing benchmark-only work from studies that also contribute a mitigation. We then located the protected operation and the evaluation setting independently of the guarantee type. The direct code D requires evidence that protection covers a persistent update in a workflow serving one user over time. Prototype and simulated histories qualify with the evaluation setting noted. The surface Supplementary Material: Privacy Protection in Self-Evolving Personal Agents 55 

code S applies when the personal workflow’s reported protection concerns reading or disclosure. The transfer code T applies to eligible operators evaluated outside those target settings. When an architecture supports multiple operations, an operation can support its own code only where the paper describes and evaluates that protection. Where a stronger reading lacks direct evidence, we retain the code supported by the documented operator and setting. 

BR1. PANOLA (source #63). Baseline: representative mechanism, S. Candidate alternative: 

D, since the bidding policy persists and learns. In the archival paper, §3.1 describes learning from auction outcomes and §4.1–4.3 evaluate the sharing decisions of simulated personas. The reported protection concerns release decisions for jointly owned content. Those evalu-ations provide no protection evidence for policy updates or their feedback. Ruling: retain S

for empirical release governance. [131] 

BR2. Galaxy (S4:R02, source #25). Baseline: representative mechanism, S. Candidate alter-native: D, since the Persona and Kernel evolve. In arXiv:2508.03991v1, §3 and Fig. 4 place masking before cloud transmission. The comparison in §4, Table 1 removes the entire Kernel, so it cannot isolate protection of the internal update calls. Ruling: retain S for context release in a personal agent and limit the empirical claim to the evaluated configuration. [6] 

BR3. Opal (source #9). Baseline: representative mechanism, D. Candidate alternatives: S if treated as private retrieval, or T if only live-user deployment qualified. In arXiv:2604.02522v1, §2.1–2.2 and §3.3–3.4 cover ingestion and maintenance under a stated host leakage model. Sections 4 and 6 describe a synthetic longitudinal personal workload and prototype evalu-ation. Ruling: retain D for confidential maintenance. Record synthetic-workload evidence separately from the conditional TEE/ORAM guarantee. [67] 

BR4. SP-Mem (source #39). Baseline: extended mechanism, D. Candidate alternative: S if only the consent gate is considered. In arXiv:2608.16551v1, §2.1–2.2 and Appendix D.1, Algorithm 3 specify sanitization before writing searchable memory and a separate store for exact values. Section 4.2 evaluates the writing step on constructed histories. Section 2.3 and Algorithm 4 cover consent at retrieval. Ruling: retain D on the basis of write-time protection. The study remains an extended example of representation and storage separation, with consent-based retrieval discussed separately. [139] 

BR5. SBU (S4:R01, source #24). Baseline: representative mechanism, T. Candidate alter-native: D, since the withdrawal operator modifies both memory and parameters. In arXiv:2602.17692v2, §3.2 and Algorithm 1 specify dual-path cleanup. Section 4.1 and the Ethical Statement identify public medical question-answering data. The reported setting does not follow the same user’s personal history across sessions. Ruling: retain 

T for transferable empirical unlearning. Its closed loop does not establish target-setting evidence. [133] 

BR6. FedSkillGit (source #17). Baseline: representative mechanism, T. An earlier draft con-sidered D because clients modify persistent skills across rounds. In arXiv:2610.21348v1, §III-A–C locates privacy filtering on transmitted client commits. The server evolves a shared skill, and §IV-C.2 and Table III measure first-pass payload audit performance. Ruling: con-firm the earlier correction to T. The protected operation serves a federated shared skill, and the ablation supports a payload-filtering claim. [73] 

BR7. Tobaben et al. (source #36). Baseline: risk and evaluation primary role, with mecha-nism code T. Candidate alternative: risk only, since the paper exposes an output-label side channel. In arXiv:2411.04680v6, §4.1 describes that side channel, §4.2–4.3 specify private label release and a data-independent label space, and §6 evaluates continual classifiers. 56 Zibin Lin, Taotao Wang, and Shengli Zhang 

Ruling: retain the risk role for the side-channel finding and T for the transferable mitigation. The source contributes once to the corpus total. [128] 

BR8. AUBench (S4:R03). Initial decision: E4 for mechanism coding, with a contextual ci-tation. Candidate alternative: T because the evaluation covers coordinated memory and parameter forgetting. In arXiv:2610.17659v1, §4.1–4.2 define a benchmark with controlled facts across weights, memory, and tools. The experiments and Appendix A.1 compare unlearning methods within that benchmark. Ruling: retain E4 because the contribution used here is the evaluation protocol and its tests. Stage 4 retains E4 for mechanisms and includes AUBench in the risk and evaluation stratum (#81 in Table S9). [79] 

Outcome. The eight decisions retain the original operator-distance rulings. The Stage 4 recheck changes AUBench’s corpus inclusion while preserving its mechanism disposition. It adds two dual-coded risk studies, because they contribute identifiable interventions in addition to evaluation evidence. AfterDeleteBench modifies persistent state in a controlled, synthetic loop and receives 

D. Attribution Shield is evaluated on query reformulation and a memory-extraction proxy and receives T. The Stage 5 follow-up adds MemJoin as a dual-coded risk study with S for its tested read-view intervention. The other additions and their boundaries appear in Section S1.6 and Table S9. These decision checks establish source-level traceability. They do not estimate independent coding agreement. 

Stage 7 operator rulings. PrivMeSA moves from D to T. The parameter intervention trains shared LoRA offline (§3.3 and §4.1); persistent lesson writes use a separate frozen distiller (§3.4 and App. C.1); and the release evaluation measures outgoing messages and cross-visit linkage (§4.1 and App. B). The test-time setting holds weights fixed and adds reusable lessons across patients (App. E.1). Each is T in this review’s single-user scope. The primary role remains representative, counted once [134]. Stage 7 provisionally credited MaskClaw’s controlled admission as D. The Stage 10 source check changes that ruling. ConMem remains T: it evaluates candidate decisions from reconstructed histories without carrying the resulting memory across successive writes [173]. 

Stage 10 adjudication: MaskClaw, arXiv v2. Section 4.5 and Eq. (9) specify AuditMerge into local state. Section 5.5 and App. E (Tables 18–19) document curated corrections, optimizer iterations, and held-out skill-use tests in five scenarios; the arbitration-rule store is fixed. Section 6.4 and App. F (Table 20) measure candidate-level pass rates under the text and sandbox gates. App. K (Table 28) identifies the sandbox judge as an external multimodal endpoint. These are positive results for candidate behavior and screening. They do not identify a committed version loaded and used in a subsequent interaction by the same user. An optimization iteration is not such an interaction. 

Ruling: change E3 from D/L to T/B. Keep the separate E1/E2 screen-disclosure tests at T/B: they test static GUI observations, not the release of retained personal state after adaptation. No D credit is assigned to either evaluation [178]. 

S3.2 Direct Evidence and Representative-Set Sensitivity 

The revised corpus has nine D sources. Table S22 gives the operation-level coding, including separately tested interfaces for the same source. 

Write, storage, and derivation, five sources. MemPrivacy tests typed writeback masking on memory benchmarks including PersonaMem-v2 (v3, §§4.1 and 5.3). HealthJev tests selective writeback over simulated health interactions (v2, memory policy and longitudi-nal evaluation). SP-Mem tests pre-write sanitization and separate exact-value storage on constructed histories (v1, §§2.1–2.3 and 4.2). Kaneko et al. test summary anonymization Supplementary Material: Privacy Protection in Self-Evolving Personal Agents 57 

in multi-session support simulations (v2, §§2.5 and 3.5, Fig. 4). Audience-Bound Persistent Memory tests ingest and derived audience constraints in controlled histories (v1, §§3.1–3.5 and 6.4) [18, 65, 74, 85, 139]. 

Local profile maintenance, one source. HomeLLaMA specifies a local profile-update and retrieval path and evaluates carryover with ten participants (§§IV-D, VI-B.1, VII-B and Fig. 16). Its privacy scope is direct profile transmission to the cloud. Command obfuscation and cloud fallback frequency are evaluated separately [60]. 

Maintenance, one source. Opal evaluates an enclave and ORAM prototype on synthetic personal workloads, with ingest and background updates inside the stipulated host leakage model (v1, §§2–4 and 6) [67]. 

Withdrawal, two sources. Reglos maintains per-user facts and invalidation in its persistent memory system and reports benchmark behavior (v1, §§3.1 and 4). AfterDeleteBench retires or rebuilds recorded descendants in a synthetic loop with fixed weights and complete annotated lineage (v1, §§3–4) [106, 162]. We substituted scope-challenging sources from Stage 7 into the 22-row comparison at neighboring operations and checked whether the replacement changed the carrier and guarantee conclusions: 

HealthJev to ConMem. Direct selective admission becomes transferable candidate screen-ing. The revised set has (𝐷, 𝑆,𝑇 ) = (4, 4, 14 ) and the substitution gives (3, 4, 15 ). MemPrivacy still supplies direct writeback evidence [18, 74, 173]. 

MaskClaw to P2Skill. The count stays (4, 4, 14 ). The emphasis shifts from scenario-level candidate screening to offline refinement observed by a cloud evaluator. Neither supplies direct evidence for protected persistent skill reuse [105, 178]. 

PrivMeSA to PeCL. The count remains (4, 4, 14 ). The replacement shifts the unit from disclosure-risk optimization on clinical cohorts to sensitive token embeddings in a task-incremental benchmark. Both leave per-user parameter adaptation untested [134, 168]. 

SBU to Secure Forgetting. The count remains (4, 4, 14 ). Both use public tasks; the supported claim changes from joint memory and parameter mitigation to specified state, trajectory, and environment behavior relative to a reference. Neither substitution supplies a longitudinal personal erasure guarantee [133, 164]. With all four substitutions, the count is (3, 4, 15 ). These substitutions test exemplar choice within the screened corpus, not search recall or independent agreement.

S4 Extended Protocols and Tabular Results for Mechanism Diagnostics 

This section documents the complete protocols, execution-quality ledgers, multi-layer observation contingencies, full-grid parameter trajectories, state-scale closure topologies, offline trace-omission configurations, and anonymized reproducibility specifications for the bounded mechanism diag-nostics summarized in the main text. Across all diagnostic batches, protocols were frozen prior to execution, evaluated exclusively with synthetic markers or public offline preference features, and analyzed strictly within each batch’s designated experimental unit without cross-batch pooling or population-level statistical inference. 

S4.1 Protocol Specifications, Execution Quality, and Computational Cost 

Four diagnostic suites operationalize the update-operator, protection-unit, and withdrawal distinc-tions developed in the review: (1) Fixed 12-Episode Synthetic-Marker Withdrawal Diagnostic ( formal_protocol_v1 

and revision_v2_formal_12x2 ). Twelve synthetic episodes (each containing five initial records and one or two target source records 𝑅 ) are instantiated under two fixed build seeds (b1 : 42; b2 : 43), yielding 24 paired episode-builds that represent repeated builds of the same synthetic cases rather than 24 independent users. For each episode and build seed, full-state retention ( K), direct source-root deletion ( D), and observed read-to-write successor-closure deletion ( L) restore the same pre-intervention snapshot. The executor verifies their digests before deletion, and D and L use the same deletion operation. The reference arm ( N) builds a fresh store from the target-free source history for that episode and seed. Each branch is probed immediately after intervention ( 𝑡 0) and after five pre-frozen, target-free exogenous follow-up updates ( 𝑡 1), using two target probes ( target_direct , target_rephrased ) and one non-target exact-field control probe ( utility ), totaling 24 ×4×2×3 = 576 logical probes per run. Primary exact-marker recoverability 𝑌 ∈ { 0, 1} equals 1 if either target probe’s raw output contains the complete synthetic marker, 0 if both target probes are technically valid and negative, and missing (bounds [0, 1]) otherwise. Run v1 and prospectively revised Run v2 use separate manifests and ledgers; v2 raised the native evolution output cap from 2,048 to 4 ,096 tokens (retaining 256 for the evidence reader within a 32 ,768-token context window) and recorded pure native invalid_link events as audited degradations rather than group-fatal aborts. The two runs are never pooled. (2) Label-Private Logistic Parameter Update Grid (Stage A: run_synthetic_v2 and run_ movielens_v1 ). A 𝑑 = 16 projected logistic preference model ( ℓ2 gradient clipping thresh-old 𝐶 = 0.25, step size 𝜂 = 0.5, parameter projection radius 4 .0) is trained over 𝑁 = 64 fixed training feature slots under label-replacement adjacency of 𝑘 ∈ { 1, 4, 16 , 64 } private label slots ( Δ𝑘 = 2𝑘𝐶 /𝑁 , 𝛿 = 10 −6). Gaussian noise is calibrated analytically via Rényi differential privacy (RDP) sequential composition ( 𝜌 = 𝑇 Δ2 

> 𝑘

/( 2𝜎 2)) and conversion (opti-mizing (𝛼, 𝛼𝜌 )-RDP over 𝛼 > 1 at 𝛼 ∗ = 1 +√︁ log (1/𝛿 )/ 𝜌 > 1 to yield the upper bound 

𝜀 ≤ 𝜌 + 2√︁ 𝜌 log (1/𝛿 ), which is conservative relative to exact Gaussian hockey-stick diver-gence); IEEE-754 closed-form inversion 𝜌 = (√︁ log (1/𝛿 ) + 𝜀 −√︁ log (1/𝛿 )) 2 is verified against numerical back-checks with maximum absolute error 7 .11 × 10 −15 . The synthetic suite eval-uates 24 fixed preference profiles across 𝑇 ∈ { 1, 2, 4, 8, 16 , 32 }, 𝜀 ∈ { 0.5, 1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 8, 16 }, and five noise seeds ( {701 , 907 , 1213 , 1601 , 2027 }), totaling 25 ,488 non-zero update trajectories (23 ,040 fixed-budget, 2 ,160 fixed-𝜎 , and 288 deterministic no-noise baselines) plus 24 zero-update baselines (267 ,624 optimizer steps). The public anchor evaluates 24 MovieLens 100K users ( ≥ 84 ratings each; ratings ≥ 4 mapped to +1 and ≤ 3 to −1 over fixed projections of genre metadata, 20 evaluation pairs per user) at 𝑇 = 16 across the same (𝑘, 𝜀 ) grid and three 60 Zibin Lin, Taotao Wang, and Shengli Zhang 

noise seeds ( {701 , 907 , 1213 }), totaling 2 ,352 non-zero update trajectories (2 ,304 fixed-budget and 48 deterministic baselines) plus 24 zero-update baselines (37 ,632 optimizer steps), yield-ing 27 ,840 non-zero training trajectories overall. The MovieLens label guarantee conditions on the selected user, the timestamp-ordered item slots, and their projected features. The held-out item–label pairs are fixed for evaluation. The condition excludes user selection, timing and item history from the protected changes. The proof analyzes noise draws private to the randomized mechanism, with the released head as its output. Benchmark runners fix seeds for reproducibility, and the archive reports accuracy aggregates without a separate DP guarantee for those releases. An accompanying analytical calibration suite ( survey_ accounting ) independently verifies post-processing invariance under repeated display ( 𝑃 0), linear RDP accumulation ( 𝑃 1), Monte Carlo mean-squared error agreement (100 repeats), and a deterministic adjacency counterexample for noiseless clipped sample means ( 𝑃 2). (3) Four-History Memory Cleanup and Source-Reintroduction Diagnostic (Stage B: 

run_dev_v3 ). Four fixed 12-record synthetic histories with inductive synthesis updates (dev_h0 –dev_h3 ) are built five times ( dev_h2 built under both b1 and b2 , averaged within history prior to equal-weight aggregation across the four histories). Primary arms K, D,

L, M1 (cardinality-matched random deletion, which removes 12 /12 records here because conservative closures saturate all five builds), and N are evaluated at 𝑡 0 and after five continued updates ( 𝑡 5, matching the five-update horizon labeled 𝑡 1 in Formal v2 ) across five probes per checkpoint ( target_direct , target_rephrased , near_field , far_field ,

structured_task ). Single-unit calibration controls on dev_h2/b1 additionally inspect fractional deletion ( 𝛼 = 0.5, removing 6 /12 records across L and matched random arms M1 –

M3 ) and partial trace retention ( 𝑝 = 0.5, retaining 81 /163 edges). Longitudinal trajectory E2 

tracks dev_h2/b1 across update steps 𝑡 6, 𝑡 10 , 𝑡 15 , 𝑡 20 under a normal target-free path and an explicit source-reintroduction path (re-submitting withdrawn source source:dev_h2:t1 

at 𝑡 10 ), adding arm G (L plus an explicit withdrawn-source registry gate). Total Stage B evaluation comprises 480 isolated-process probes (192 target probes and 288 utility/task probes) and 275 follow-up events. (4) State-Scale Dependency Closure and Offline Trace-Omission Diagnostic (Stage C: 

run_scale_v1 ). Eight single-seed ( b1 : 42) synthetic A-MEM states cross four sizes ( |𝑆 | ∈ {12 , 24 , 48 , 96 }) with two write orderings ( blocked vs. interleaved over identical record sets, 360 total ingested records, zero answer probes). Subsequently, an offline structural ablation evaluates 336 trace-retention configurations across the eight frozen read-to-write graphs at seven edge-retention fractions 𝑝 ∈ { 0, 0.125 , 0.25 , 0.5, 0.75 , 0.875 , 1} (⌊𝑝 |𝐸 |⌋ re-tained edges) under nested random edge omission (five permutation seeds {11 , 23 , 37 , 53 , 71 }

per state) and centered contiguous log gaps. Table S12 reports execution completeness, token volume, and wall-clock cost across all batches without pooling denominators. pilot_06/b2 , pilot_11/b1 ), and three produced out-of-range native link indices ( CONNECTIONS: 0, 2 against two shown records) that the strict v1 adapter escalated into fatal state errors (pilot_09/b1 , pilot_10/b2 , pilot_12/b1 ), leaving 108 missing probes and wide all-assignment missing-data bounds for 𝑌 D − 𝑌 L at 𝑡 1 ([− 0.2083 , 0.2917 ]; 1 /18 = 0.0556 among complete pairs). S4.2 Multi-Layer Observation Surface and Reader-Guard Audit 

Because suppression at an output verifier does not imply erasure from persistent state or non-retrieval during context assembly, Stage B records four binary indicators per target probe: persistent state presence ( state_present , whether the exact marker remains in any active record or evolved context field), retrieval exposure ( retrieval_hit , whether a marker-bearing record enters the retrieved prompt context), raw generation recovery ( raw_hit , whether the unconstrained model output contains the exact marker), and post-guard target delivery ( guarded_hit , alongside binary 

guard_accepted ). Crucially, guarded_hit=0 and guard_accepted=0 are distinct events: when no target is generated ( raw_hit=0 ) and the model emits a schema-valid null abstinence with consistent evidence metadata, the reader guard accepts the negative response ( guard_accepted=1 ,

verified_missing ). Table S14 presents the joint contingency distribution across all 192 Stage B target probes (evidence_joint_patterns.csv ). Exactly half (96 /192) are clean negatives across all four layers (L, M1 , N, and blocked G/E2 checkpoints), and 91 /192 (47 .40%) are positive across all four layers. Five target probes exhibit divergence across layers—all within source-only deletion arm D: two target_ direct probes ( dev_h0/b1 at 𝑡 0 and 𝑡 5) retrieve marker-bearing evolved context into the prompt (state=1 , retrieval=1 ) without emitting the marker in raw output ( raw=0 , guard_accepted=1 ), whereas three target_rephrased probes emit the exact marker in raw output ( raw=1 ) but are rejected by the citation/consistency guard ( guard_accepted=0 , guarded_hit=0 ). In dev_h0/b1 at 

𝑡 5, counting only guard-accepted answers would misclassify D as negative on that history (75 .0% four-history mean), whereas retaining raw hits captures the 100 .0% persistent survival of the marker across all four histories. Table S15 audits every reader-guard rejection across both Stage B (4 /480 Supplementary Material: Privacy Protection in Self-Evolving Personal Agents 63 

probes) and Formal v2 (11 /576 probes across seven episode-build/arm/checkpoint cells; all 576 and 480 probes had schema_valid=true and unchanged SHA-256 state digests).

Across the synthetic grid, deterministic ℓ2 clipping at 𝐶 = 0.25 without noise yields an invariant 80 .8% ± 5.0% balanced accuracy across all 𝑇 ∈ { 1, 2, 4, 8, 16 , 32 } (compared with 80 .8%–81 .4% unclipped), con-firming that utility degradation in Table S17 is driven by group sensitivity Δ𝑘 = 2𝑘𝐶 /𝑁 rather than clipping bias; mean gradient clipping fractions range from 0 .858 to 1 .000 and mean test log-loss increases from 0 .632 ( 𝑇 = 32 , 𝑘 = 1, 𝜀 = 16) to 0 .837 ( 𝑇 = 32 , 𝑘 = 64 , 𝜀 = 4). On MovieLens 100K, class imbalance elevates zero-update raw accuracy to 62 .7% while balanced accuracy sits at 50 .0%, and the 𝑇 = 16 clipped no-noise baseline reaches 56 .3% ± 12 .6% balanced accuracy, bounding all noisy curves near the public genre-only linear ceiling. 64 Zibin Lin, Taotao Wang, and Shengli Zhang

S4.4 State-Scale Dependency Closure and Offline Trace-Edge Omission 

Table S18 reports the full structural topology of the eight single-seed Stage C A-MEM states (figure5_state_scale.csv ), supplementing the main text with maximum source-to-descendant hop distance, total recorded read-to-write trace edges |𝐸 |, non-root reachable descendant denom-inator |𝐶 \ 𝑅 |, and exact filler-record identifiers for the two native invalid_link degradations (t_fill_039 in 48-blocked and t_fill_083 in 96-interleaved ). Note that total trace edges |𝐸 |

increase with state size and interleaved evolution (45 to 827 edges), whereas conservative reachable closure in 96-interleaved drops to 5 /96 (5 .21%, maximum hop distance 3, |𝐶 \ 𝑅 | = 4) when target ingestion occurs at write position 14. Because write ordering and target position vary jointly under a single build seed, these values establish structural non-monotonicity without isolating causal main effects. Table S19 presents all 336 offline trace-omission configurations ( trace_ablation.csv ) across the eight frozen graphs and seven retention levels 𝑝 ∈ { 0, 0.125 , 0.25 , 0.5, 0.75 , 0.875 , 1}. By listing the five individual random-omission seed missed counts ( 𝑠 ∈ { 11 , 23 , 37 , 53 , 71 }) alongside their within-state mean (SD) and the deterministic centered contiguous-gap count, the table losslessly encodes every configuration (56 × 5 + 56 × 1 = 336 rows). At intermediate retention levels ( 𝑝 = 0.5and 𝑝 = 0.75), contiguous logging outages sever entire temporal evolution chains and therefore miss substantially more reachable descendants than dispersed random omissions of identical edge count (79 .0% vs. 36 .9% at 𝑝 = 0.5, Δ = +42 .1 pp; 51 .5% vs. 13 .7% at 𝑝 = 0.75, Δ = +37 .8 pp).

S4.5 Double-Blind Reproducibility and Artifact Verification Notes 

To support independent verification while preserving double-blind review anonymity and strict data-protection boundaries, the companion reproducibility packager ( package_anonymized_ artifact.py ) constructs and verifies CSUR_Reproducibility_Artifact_Anonymous.zip under five engineering and governance rules: (1) Repository-Relative Anonymized Layout and Dual-Digest Manifest. The packager maps source workspace directories ( survey_runtime_2026-11-11/ , survey_extension_ v5_2026-11-17/ , and survey_accounting_2026-11-11/ ) into three self-contained archive modules— survey_runtime/ (Formal v1 /v2 12-episode synthetic-marker adapters, frozen protocols, and figure_ready_results/ ), survey_extension_v5/ (Stage Aparameter grids, Stage B 4-history memory cleanup and E2 reintroduction, Stage C 8-state scale pilot, and organized_results_2026-11-17/ ), and survey_accounting/ (analytical Gaussian RDP composition and Monte Carlo calibration). The archive README reports the packaged file count. The archive root contains README.md , verify_offline.py ,and sanitized_manifest.json . Across the 10 sanitized configuration, protocol, and analysis-manifest files (runtime_config*.json , formal_protocol.json , prepare_ protocol.py , and analysis_manifest.json ), workstation-specific filesystem paths are rewritten to <LOCAL_EMBEDDING_DIR>/all-MiniLM-L6-v2 and repository-relative 

survey_extension_v5/ prefixes. To preserve auditability against the five historical 

freeze_manifest.json records, sanitized_manifest.json pairs each pre-sanitization SHA-256 digest with its post-sanitization archive digest and verifies unchanged digests for all remaining frozen files. (2) Pinned Upstream Baseline and Deterministic Local Inference. Memory diagnostics pin the open-source MIT-licensed A-MEM baseline to upstream commit f9a173de c325d84fcc97e7828775c7b241c33466 , with Formal v2 , Stage B, and Stage C applying the prospective patch revision_v2/amem_upstream_v2.patch to log and isolate pure 

invalid_link events without clamping or remapping indices. Third-party upstream clones ( amem/upstream/ ) are excluded from archive redistribution and cloned separately per survey_runtime/README.md prior to running test_structure.py . All LLM-backed runs bind exclusively to a project-isolated loopback Ollama endpoint ( http://127.0.0.1: 11435 ) serving ministral-3:8b (pinned SHA-256 digest 1922accd5827ebe6829e5363 69195db25eaf664528dc66206d646ea3bb386b71 ) with temperature=0 , num_ctx=32768 ,single-concurrency GPU execution, fixed build seeds ( b1=42 , b2=43 ), zero external network egress, and zero paid API calls. Stage B development ran on an idle identical-capacity workstation GPU ( GPU1 , 32 GiB cap) documented as a pre-submission resource allocation note while keeping formal hardware freezes separate. (3) Process-Isolated Non-Mutating Probe Execution. Every memory read probe executes in a fresh worker subprocess from a serialized state snapshot and verifies exact SHA-256 state-digest equality before and after probe execution (576 /576 in Formal v2 ; 480 /480 in Stage B), ensuring that evaluation queries cannot mutate persistent memory, neighbor links, or subsequent checkpoints. Independent cold-restart workers (96 /96 in Formal v2 ; 25 /25 in Stage B) further confirm deterministic snapshot restoration. (4) Synthetic-Marker Hygiene and Excluded Raw Artifacts. Memory histories, target values, and control probes use purely synthetic markers containing zero real user records or personally identifiable information. The packager strictly excludes model weights and local embedding checkpoints, raw multi-megabyte LLM backend call transcripts ( backend_ 68 Zibin Lin, Taotao Wang, and Shengli Zhang 

calls.jsonl , probes.jsonl , ollama.log ) and serialized runtime memory states (retain-ing compact Stage C trace.json lineage edge graphs and hash-locked structured CSV/JSON ledgers), raw MovieLens 100K rating files and sweep intermediates, and macOS AppleDou-ble ( ._* ) sidecar files. For Stage A MovieLens 100K experiments, the archive distributes deterministic feature-projection and evaluation scripts (seed 20261117 ) alongside aggregate CSV summaries; reproducers obtain raw MovieLens 100K ratings directly from the official GroupLens release. (5) Offline Verification Inside a Temporary Unpack Directory. Invoking python3 package_anonymized_artifact.py –output build/CSUR_Reproducibility_ Artifact_Anonymous.zip –verify (or running python3 verify_offline.py after extracting the archive) executes deterministic CPU verification using packaged data and the dependencies listed in the archive README: (i) zero host-path and sidecar hygiene scans alongside the complete sanitized_manifest.json and five-manifest freeze-hash verification; (ii) structural, denominator, and claim-boundary checks matching validate_ organized_results.py together with all 17 /17 SHA-256 digests in organized_results_ 2026-11-17/artifact-manifest.json (invoking validate_organized_results.py 

directly on its raw-source re-hash step requires the omitted raw probes.jsonl /

backend_calls.jsonl logs and sweep files); (iii) Stage C eight-state trace.json closure recomputation and recomputation of the 336 packaged trace-omission rows; and (iv) all 11 offline pytest /unittest /validate_protocol.py suites ( test_reader across four adapter snapshots, test_metrics , validate_protocol.py , test_synthetic_ parameter_update , test_movielens_parameter_update , test_structural_controls ,

test_dev_data , test_reader_v5 , test_runtime_controls , test_scale_data , and 

test_accounting ). 

Offline verification and model reruns. The archive is ready for offline verification after extraction. The historical model runner checks the original freeze digests and rejects the sanitized runtime_ config.json . To prepare a local rerun, first verify the extracted archive against its sanitized manifest. Then use a separate working copy, restore the pinned upstream revision and patch, provision the local model and embeddings, and configure the local paths. Record those inputs in a separate rerun manifest and use a runner that validates it. The archive retains the original freeze and sanitized digests for provenance. The supplied historical runner does not implement the local rerun path. 

S4.6 Fixed Synthetic-Marker Diagnostic: Deletion Scope versus Utility Retention 

To illustrate the withdrawal claims in Main Table 5, we run a fixed synthetic-marker diagnostic on a versioned A-MEM store across 12 synthetic episodes (5 initial records each) × 2 deterministic build seeds, yielding 24 paired episode-builds (Tables S12–S13). Within each episode-build, keep (K), root-only deletion ( D), and conservative observed read-to-write closure deletion ( L) start from the same saved snapshot. We verify their state digests before intervention. The reference arm ( N)uses the same episode and build seed to construct a fresh store from a target-free source history. All four arms are probed at 𝑡 0 and after five target-free updates ( 𝑡 1, called 𝑡 5 in Stage B). The 576 probes cover both checkpoints. All 24 episode-builds completed. All 576 probes returned schema-valid JSON and preserved the state digest. The evidence guard accepted 565 probes and rejected 11. Raw-output scoring retains the rejected responses. Raw target hits are 24/24 ( K), 3/24 ( D), 0/24 ( L), and 0/24 ( N) at 𝑡 0, and 24/24 ( K), 1/24 ( D), 0/24 ( L), and 0/24 ( N) at 𝑡 1 (Figure S1B). Non-target exact-field retention at 𝑡 1

is 21/24 ( K, 3 missing), 16/24 ( D, losses concentrated where target and control fields co-occurred Supplementary Material: Privacy Protection in Self-Evolving Personal Agents 69 

in the root record), 0/24 ( L), and 22/24 ( N). Because conservative closures saturated all five initial records, L eliminated tested initial personalization alongside target recovery, and D retained 16/24 tested fields with 1/24 post-update raw recovery. With three unresolved utility readings in K, its retention is identified only within 87.5–100%. The comparison measures the cost of saturation. AfterDeleteBench isolates a tracked descendant under a complete harness, and Bemula conditions preservation on paired controls [106, 151]. A comparison of selective cleanup in this store would need both partial deletion and retained utility measured on the same states. A How far can dependencies extend? S4.7 Extended Diagnostics: Protection Units, Cleanup Scope, and Source Reintroduction 

Stage A tests a logistic preference head on 24 synthetic profiles and 24 MovieLens 100K users. Across settings, baselines, and repeated noise seeds, there are 27,840 non-zero training trajectories (Tables S16–S17). The randomized mechanism protects changes to up to 𝑘 binary labels in 𝑁 = 64 fixed training slots under approximate (𝜀, 𝛿 )-DP ( 𝛿 = 10 −6, ℓ2 clip 𝐶 = 0.25, sensitivity Δ𝑘 = 2𝑘𝐶 /𝑁 )for 𝑘 ∈ { 1, 4, 16 , 64 } protected slots ( 𝑘 = 64 permits replacing all 64 labels). For MovieLens, the guarantee conditions on the selected user, ordered item slots, and projected genre features. The held-out item–label pairs are fixed in the comparison. User selection and item history fall outside this label adjacency. The guarantee concerns the randomized head under private noise draws. We use fixed seeds for the benchmark and report aggregate accuracy. These reported aggregates have no separate release guarantee. At 𝑇 = 16 and 𝜀 = 16 (Figure S2), widening 𝑘 from 1 to 4 , 16 , and 64 drops synthetic balanced accuracy from 79 .1% to 69 .8%, 57 .2%, and 52 .0%. On MovieLens 100K (clipped 70 Zibin Lin, Taotao Wang, and Shengli Zhang 

baseline 56 .3% using genre metadata), 𝜀 = 16 accuracies are 55 .6%, 54 .9%, 50 .2%, and 50 .7%. These are descriptive comparisons at one budget and update count. The synthetic means decline across this slice, while the MovieLens means lie below the clipped baseline and vary nonmonotonically. Stage B tests four 12-record synthetic histories across five builds ( dev_h2 built twice, averaged first), adding cardinality-matched random deletion M1 . This development-stage batch did not advance to formal 24-history expansion because buffered calls exceeded the pre-specified budget gate (1 ,909 × 1.30 = 2,481 .7 > 2,400). Section S4.1 identifies the protocol and acceptance records. At five updates ( 𝑡 5, aligned with 𝑡 1 in Section S4.6; Figure S3), raw target recovery is 100% for 

K, D versus 0% for L, M1 , N; near-field, far-field, and task utility reach 100% for K, D, N versus 0% for L, M1 under 12 /12 saturation. At that dose both arms remove the same entire initial store. A comparison that isolates provenance targeting would need non-saturated states and deletion arms that remove the same number of records. Across 480 probes (476 guard-accepted), three rejected D

probes emitted raw target markers. At 𝑡 5, history-weighted recovery for D is 75% at the guarded output and 100% at the raw output. The two rates describe different observation interfaces. On 

dev_h2/b1 (𝑡 6–𝑡 20 ; Figure S1D), re-supplying the retracted source at 𝑡 10 restores target hits (0 → 1) for L and N, and the registry in G (L plus source registry) rejected that exact source identifier in the tested replay. 

S4.8 Memory Scale, Write Ordering, and Conservative Dependency Closure 

Stage C tests closure saturation across eight single-seed ( seed=42 , temperature 0) A-MEM states of 12 , 24 , 48 , and 96 records under blocked versus interleaved write orderings (360 writes, zero probes; telemetry in Tables S12, S18–S19). Every state keeps one exact content hit, whereas closures span 5–96 records (Table S20, Figure S1A): all blocked builds saturate (100 .0%), while 

96-interleaved drops to 5 /96 (5 .2% after 91 .7% , 100 .0% , 100 .0%), leaving non-saturated dele-tion utility untested. Because write ordering and target arrival index co-vary ( blocked pos. 1 vs. interleaved pos. 2 , 4, 7, 14) under one seed, causal ordering effects remain confounded. S5 Operation-Level Evidence Matrix S5.1 Unit and decision rules 

The matrix indexes a source, a specific protective operation, and its reported evaluation or formal setting. Source identifiers refer to the pinned references in Table S9. We split a study when the protected view, intervention, or setting changes. For example, Reglos’s tombstone update and reader policy share a joint behavioral result, while its manual deletion control has a narrower documented scope. If a test reports a pipeline outcome, the corresponding rows identify that shared outcome and leave the component effect unassigned. Benchmark controls without an identifiable protection step remain in Table S9. Table S11 supplies fuller trust assumptions and primary-text locators for the 22 exemplars. For an individual row, D requires evidence that a protected update affects durable single-user state carried across decisions or interactions. A candidate revision within an optimization run is not evidence of persistence. S denotes protection evaluated at a read or release interface over retained personal state, including fixed profiles instantiated in personal-agent tasks. T denotes an adjacent setting or a transferable primitive. The same source can contribute more than one code. A dash 72 Zibin Lin, Taotao Wang, and Shengli Zhang 

marks a feature whose scope is described but whose protection claim has no separately creditable outcome or applicable formal bound. The source-level tally in Table S10 uses the closest credited code ( D before S before T) once per source. Setting tags describe the evidence independently of distance. F denotes a formal result within its stated assumptions. B is a fixed-dataset, isolated-component, or replay benchmark. L is a controlled longitudinal personal history or simulated workflow with evidence of state carryover. E is a field study or production deployment. I marks design or implementation evidence without a reported protection result. A dash in the distance column excludes the row from protection counts; for example, — / E records a field setting without a supported privacy outcome. 

S5.2 Coverage and deduplication 

Table S21 counts coded rows and gives distinct source counts in parentheses. A study can appear in multiple families and distance columns. The D evidence covers memory write or maintenance (seven sources) and memory withdrawal (two), all in controlled settings. HomeLLaMA includes real participants in a lab protocol. The skill operations were evaluated in transfer settings. The matrix additionally retains uncredited design or deployment rows.
