S6 Primary Evidence for the Deployment Comparisons 

Main Section 7 compares histories with and without a private evening-booking restriction while holding the public task, slots, and permissions fixed. The sources below evaluate different settings. The booking comparisons identify their observation interfaces and the assumptions needed to transfer each result. 

S6.1 Booking: Host Trace, Recipient Transcript, and Visible Action 

State and recipient comparators. The Always-On Evaluation Protocol (v1, §§9.2–9.4, Table 18) reconstructs state from an event stream and checks authority epochs, deletion propagation, scope, and approval of external actions [29]. VMG (EMNLP archival, §5, Eqs. 3 and 5, Appendix D) uses principal-scoped retrieval and a query distribution over which to probe reconstruction after dele-tion [80]. Its expanded version (arXiv:2604.16548v5, §5 and Appendix D) also specifies authorized utility and paired deletion comparisons. In the booking case, use these checks at the state and retrieval interfaces. At the service interface, compare request text, selected slot, refusals, and timing across both histories before withdrawal and under the same permitted follow-up queries afterward. Score distinguishing the presence of the restriction, booking completion, and target recovery separately. For example, returning a valid evening booking only with the unrestricted history is sufficient for a distinguishing event even when both bookings satisfy authorization and the restriction is absent from message text. 

Prompt and preference. DP-OPT (ICLR 2024, §3 and Algorithms 1–2) uses local data for generation and exponential selection over a disjoint validation split [57]. Ray Chowdhury et al. (Definitions 2.2– 2.3, §§4–5) protect the comparison label, while Cho and Sun (§2.2, §3.2, Algorithm 1) privatize a reward learner operating on comparison tuples [21, 104]. For transfer, bound how often one fact occurs in each split and how many entire tuples it changes. A change in a candidate response is not label adjacency. Keep the reference sampler and online context independent of the private history except through already accounted releases. 

Memory and recipient. Opal v1 §2.2, Figure 3, and Theorem 2.1 define a host security game with public operation types, fixed trace shapes, and an exogenous checkpoint schedule. The attestation-hybrid model uses a durable monotonic client counter, and freshness of sealed client state is relative to the last provider checkpoint (§3.4) [67]. DP-MemView v1 Definition 1 (C1–C6), Theorem 1, and Corollary 1 bound adaptive application transcripts for covered same-scaffold store differences [69]. A public ingest triggered by a private fact changes the first interface. A new public slot or raw read changes the second. The booking recipient’s joint view requires coverage of every additional observable. Supplementary Material: Privacy Protection in Self-Evolving Personal Agents 85 

Matched release experiment. DP-MemView v1 §4, Table 2 holds the query trajectory and response backbone fixed. In the synthetic PairedMem pairs, the scaffold and non-target content match and 4–6 slots for the target attribute vary. With Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, 𝑇 = 16, and online DP-MemView 

𝐵 𝑎 = 2, 𝐾 = 3, the (AUC, tRec) pairs are (0.832 , 0.524 ) for TaskMin, (0.500 , 0.386 ) for TypedMask, and (0.490 , 0.506 ) for online DP-MemView. Here AUC measures the frozen auditor’s ability to distinguish histories from held-out responses and tRec scores target-required behavior via the blinded utility judge. Appendix C.4, Table 8 details the adapted baselines: TaskMin sends a fixed task-family subset and TypedMask sends slot-typed placeholders for protected items. The latter yields identical conditioning and chance AUC by construction. The same table reports public-corpus transfer AUCs of 0 .535–0 .563 across the three backbones for online DP-MemView. Compare each trace under its own corpus and observer. Changed public layouts, autonomous writes, and real booking actions need matched tests [69]. 

Mapping the history change. Let 𝐻 0 have no evening restriction and let 𝐻 1 contain one. The latter adds a labeled example to the training set and a separate labeled example to the validation set. It also replaces a neutral value in one preallocated slot in attribute group 𝐼 𝑎 . Hold slot metadata, the booking request, authority and the three publicly available slots fixed. Let 𝐺 denote the complete list of candidate prompts, 𝑃 the selected prompt, 𝑉 the augmented DP-MemView transcript and 

𝐴 the booking exchange including the request, response, timing and errors. The linked recipient view is (𝑃, 𝑉 , 𝐴 ). Raw examples, memory values, intermediate scores and 𝐺 stay at the trusted local endpoint. 

Candidate generation with stable voting groups. Use the ensemble generation and LimitedDomain selection structure of DP-OPT [57]. In this variant, set the public sampling rate to 𝑞 = 1. Partition by a fixed public map from stable record identifiers to a fixed number of groups. The assignment and order of other records are independent of the added record. Use one fixed-model vote per group with a public default vote for an empty group. At each fixed generation prefix, condition on independent per-group model randomness for that call. Only the group containing the new example can change its vote. The histogram thus changes by at most one in any coordinate and at most two coordinates change. Calibration for vote replacement can be reduced to the single-vote insertion and removal case in DP-OPT Appendix A.2.1, Theorem A.2. Apply top-one LimitedDomain with a fixed public vocabulary, deterministic tie breaking, a public domain limit and single-vote sensitivity parameters 

Δ0 = Δ∞ = 1. If its single-vote bound is (𝜂, 𝛾 ), connect the two histograms by removing the old ballot and inserting the new one. The same mechanism is defined on the intermediate histogram. The two DP inequalities give the bound (2𝜂, (1 + 𝑒 𝜂 )𝛾 ) in each direction. Choose 𝜂 = 𝜀 0/2 and 

𝛾 = 𝛿 0/( 1 + 𝑒 𝜂 ). The resulting per-call bound is (𝜀 0, 𝛿 0) for the history pair. Precommit a public candidate count and a total budget of 𝐾 private calls across the list. Every call counts, including end-of-sequence outputs, ⊥ and any retry. Termination, retry and candidate assembly use the call count and the privatized tokens only. At the cap, stop and fill any unused positions with a public default prompt. For the privacy analysis, pad the private call transcript to 𝐾 

with data-independent symbols. Each step is private for every fixed prefix, including padded steps at zero cost. Basic adaptive composition gives (𝐾𝜀 0, 𝐾𝛿 0) for that transcript and hence for 𝐺 .

Selection, reading and the recipient transcript. For each fixed 𝐺 , use the count of correct predictions on the validation set as the score and apply the exponential mechanism with exponent 𝜀 𝑣 times the score divided by two. The score changes by at most one for either history. For each fixed 𝑃 , apply DP-MemView Theorem 1 with C1–C6 on the same-scaffold stores. Future queries can depend on 𝑃 and earlier accounted outputs. For example, allocate 𝐵 𝑎 = 0.50 to two reads at 0 .25 each. Each read can select from the same two public views recommending an early or a late daytime appointment, with 86 Zibin Lin, Taotao Wang, and Shengli Zhang 

a score of one if the view matches the slot’s recommendation rule and zero otherwise. The known sensitivity is one, and each read charges attribute 𝑎 . The booking controller chooses the daytime slot from these views and public information. At this interface, request timing has a fixed public schedule, and responses and error handling use only accounted outputs and public inputs. Thus 

(𝑃, 𝑉 , 𝐴 ) is post-processing of (𝐺, 𝑃, 𝑉 ), whose sequential bound is (𝐾𝜀 0 +𝜀 𝑣 + 𝐵 𝑎 , 𝐾𝛿 0) [57, 69, 145]. As one budget example, schedule four candidate prompts with at most eight private attempts each, set 𝜀 0 = 0.025, 𝛿 0 = 2 × 10 −8 and 𝜀 𝑣 = 0.30, and use the two-read budget above. Generation contributes (0.80 , 6.4 × 10 −7) and the recipient transcript satisfies (1.60 , 6.4 × 10 −7)-DP for this pair. These are analytical budgets. The calculation uses the fixed-group generation variant, the declared recipient view and the common release schedule. 

Failure with the same recipients. Hold 𝐺 , 𝑃 , 𝑉 , the input histories and the recipient boundary fixed as mechanisms and inputs. Change the booking rule to inspect the raw restriction and choose the evening slot in 𝐻 0 and a daytime slot in 𝐻 1. If 𝐸 is the evening request at the service, Pr [𝐸 | 𝐻 0] = 1and Pr [𝐸 | 𝐻 1] = 0. The DP inequality forces 𝛿 ≥ 1. The protected prompt and read kernels still run as specified. The booking request is the output that leaves the accounted path [41, 69]. 

Failure after adding the host view. Restore the protected booking rule and extend the observer to host logs. Emit an extra host-visible ingest to an auxiliary store exactly in 𝐻 1, leaving the recipient path as specified. The ingest event now has probabilities zero and one under the two histories. The extended view requires 𝛿 ≥ 1 even though the original recipients receive the same releases as in the composable case. Opal §2.2 and Theorem 2.1 compare histories with the same public leakage. The extra ingest changes that leakage. Adding a public slot instead changes DP-MemView’s scaffold. A public schedule with the same host-visible operation counts addresses the ingest event. Bringing the full host view into the positive bound additionally requires a conditional simulation from the accounted recipient transcript and public inputs, or compatible separate accounting [67, 69]. 

Evidence on linked observations. TOP-Bench (EMNLP Findings, §§3–6) constructs 1,000 cases under a two-stage tool protocol. Individual returns are insufficient for the designated inference by construction. The study measures inference after combining returns, and evaluates prompt and training interventions for the agent’s output behavior. CIPL (v1, §§3–4, Apps. D.4 and I) distinguishes internal selection from external recovery, evaluates tools and answers separately, and contrasts a monitored tool path with an unmonitored output path. These fixed-task studies inform the observer and probe choices. The longitudinal booking comparison additionally requires a new write and reuse of the resulting state [59, 101]. 

S6.2 Adaptive Parameters and Skills: Separate Policy from Enforcement 

In the ACL archival Privacy Collapse study, §4.1 and Figure 2 compare proactive and conservative training conditions on PrivacyLens and CIMemories. The former evaluates privacy decisions in tool-use scenarios, not actual tool executions in production. Section 5.4 and Appendix I isolate partial mitigation via data filtering and mixing; there is no DP guarantee for the training data [46]. In MaskClaw v2, E3 (§5.5, §§6.3–6.4, Apps. E–F) compares text scoring, held-out skill use, and sandbox filtering. Appendix F Table 20 gives the calendar denominator (189 candidates) and rates (43.39% strict text and 16.93% sandbox). Appendix K Table 28 identifies the external judge; Section S3.1 gives the persistence ruling [178]. Audience-Bound Persistent Memory (v1, §§3.1–3.5 and 6.4) constrains context admission under its own authorization and mediation assumptions [85]. An external tool gate after generation and repeated regression checks across admitted versions are design implications, not properties established by any of these results in combination. Supplementary Material: Privacy Protection in Self-Evolving Personal Agents 87 

S6.3 Withdrawal: Match Verification to the Carrier 

Reglos v1 §3.1 and §4 (Figure 5 and forget-case analysis) support a scoped non-use claim for a tombstone combined with reader enforcement [162]. MemGov (archival, §§3.3–3.4, 4.3, Table 3, and Appendix D) certifies eligibility and pairwise consistency decisions on a bounded working set [23]. In the 202-case eligibility profile ( 𝑘 = 5), both the metadata-filter baseline (metadata/vector retrieval) and MemGov (BM25 plus governance) return zero forbidden objects. Answer hit, measured by inclusion of the expected memory ID, is 26.2% versus 70.3%. In separate pairwise profiles, unsafe merges are 22.3% versus zero observed. The certificate is local to the supplied metadata and detected constraints, and the retrieval difference prevents attributing the answer-hit gap to the repair layer alone. AfterDeleteBench v1 §5.3 and Appendix D.3, Table 6 distinguish existing descendants from future writes. For the fixed-weight Qwen IID condition (200 profiles), exact deletion gives reconstruction, commitment, and later activation of 0 .735 /0.735 /0.510. The sealed literal gate gives 0 .735 /0/0.680. Recorded-lineage closure yields 0 /0/0, and the corresponding clean-world rates are zero. The gate acts at candidate write time, so later activation can draw on surviving state even when commitment is blocked. The measurements depend on the audit interface and complete recorded lineage in the closure arm [106]. K-Bench v1 §§3–5 isolates a single source at a time (weights, prompt context, free-text retrieval, or structured retrieval) and probes six observable channels. In the structured-retrieval comparison (Section 5.3 and Figure 6b), StaR reduces recovery in the answer channel while tool observations still expose the target and the aggregate recovery rate is unchanged. This paired comparison isolates a missed observation channel under its scaffold. Combine its channel checks with a never-exposed reference and multi-carrier persistence when testing withdrawal from a personal agent [166]. The A-MEM diagnostic in Section S4.6 (Tables S12–S13 and Figure S1B) compares 24 paired episode-builds at 𝑡 1 after five target-free writes. The root-only, conservative closure, and never-exposed arms have raw exact-marker hit counts of 1, 0, and 0, and confirmed non-target exact-field recovery counts of 16, 0, and 22. The closure removes all five initial records in each build. The raw target endpoint keeps guard-rejected responses, while utility requires confirmed exact-field recovery. The equal-cardinality random deletion arm in the development run also deletes the entire initial store (Section S4.7). To isolate selective cleanup, match the deletion count in non-saturated stores and measure semantic recovery, later activation, and task utility alongside exact-marker hits. MemCut v1 Section 6.1 and Appendix B.3, Table 9 hold the budget at ten turns on a 144-fact subset spanning three systems and two write models. Widening the candidate pool from 20 to 60 reduces reported residual recovery by 12.5–23.9 percentage points for model-guided cleanup. Appendix B.4, Table 11 also compares off-target deletions on Turn RAG. These controls assess candidate reach and deletion cost at a fixed evaluation checkpoint. AfterDeleteBench measures subsequent lifecycle outcomes [106, 170]. Yao et al. v1 Theorem 1 and §5 require sanitized suffix observations that match the counterfactual execution, compatible runtime, fixed weights and deterministic decoding or fixed randomness, and the absence of committed external side effects after the target enters [163]. SBU v2 §4.3 Table 6 tests memory-only, parameter-only, and joint interventions in public medical QA [133]. SEA v1 §2.4 and §4.2 compare outputs after bypass of a preference proxy fitted on synthetic data [109]. Its deletion procedure is separate. For the booking case, probe the reloaded store, plans, and active proxy on the same tasks after recovery, and track any external booking as a separate recipient-side state. 88 Zibin Lin, Taotao Wang, and Shengli Zhang 

S6.4 Additional transfer and risk comparisons 

Training and replay. Selective DP makes sensitive token flags part of the privacy unit, and DP-Forward moves perturbation to the forward representation [32, 114]. In an adapter stack with batch-dependent precision dispatch, a changed record can affect other clipped gradients. Record-owned dispatch restores the sensitivity condition used by per-record accounting [169]. Delegated HE-LoRA keeps encrypted computations at remote workers. In GuardedTuning, split and offsite variants expose different intermediate representations to the client and provider [40, 113]. TriShield tests a separate malicious-coordinator threat in federated PEFT [144]. For single-edit streams, buffering can provide a trajectory bound with an explicit delay cost [10]. These results motivate separate checks for the unit of a released update, the compute party’s view, and the schedule of fresh private reads. 

Preference and read release. Private ordinal supervision protects a rating under fixed questionnaire inputs [100]. PREMIA and privacy-preference mixing measure membership and memorization in offline training [38, 159]. These endpoints complement behavioral disclosure tests after adaptation. For read release, PROFIT measures cloud-visible rewrites of current queries under fixed policies, proof-backed advice reveals a designated rule outcome, and COLLABMEM tests scope enforcement at a shared graph interface [103, 143, 148]. CIDER supplies user-labeled disclosure judgments for evaluating personalized policies [49]. Each study fixes something that can change inside a personal loop, such as the policy, predicate, role, or history. Tests after revision should record whether such a change affects the same release observation. 

Matched tuning and observer comparisons from the rescreening. Ma et al. compare full tuning and parameter-efficient tuning on the same tasks [91]. On IMDb with DistilBERT, non-private full tuning and LoRA have observed membership-inference AUCs of 0 .59 and 0 .52 (Figure 2). For the private variants on the same P5000, full tuning and LoRA use 8 .50 and 6 .10 GB, respectively (Table 4). These are task-specific observations. PACZero protects which subset is drawn from a stated public candidate family, with the private update sequence and its post-processed model as outputs (v3, Section 3.4, Theorem 5) [37]. Mapping that guarantee to the booking fact would require a distribution over histories and an explicit correspondence between the fact and the hidden subset. 

What crosses a training boundary. VeilTune separates the data owner’s training view from the model owner’s output adapter under its semi-honest simulation assumptions (v1, Sections 3.2 and 5.6) [153]. The output adapter has its own disclosure boundary. PrivPair’s matched split-interface evaluation measures the server-visible activations and gradients (v1, Sections III-B and VII-C–F) [62]. PrivTune measures inversion and attribute inference on perturbed token representations, with input-dependent calibration requiring a separate transcript analysis [86]. AEGIS isolates the effect of protecting several gradient components together in its single-step comparison (v1, Sections III-A and VI-C/F) [127]. These results tell the reviewer which messages enter the mechanism-specific view. Later release of model weights or linked rounds requires its own assessment. 

Conditional communication and deployment costs. pAirZero bounds a record change at the receiver of a wireless aggregate under stated channel and power assumptions (v1, Lemma 1). Its device-memory and upload figures are estimates for the modeled system (Table II) [8]. DP-DyLoRA uses a common public rank within a round and accounts for user contributions through the aggregate. Large-cohort costs and accuracy are evaluated under simulated noise conditions (arXiv:2405.06368v4, Sections V-A, VI-F and VII) [155]. HE-OFT compares score and label-only inference from the same trained head, with measured latency and model recovery under the tested configurations (v1, Section V-E/F, Tables 7 and 10). Section IV-F distinguishes those measurements Supplementary Material: Privacy Protection in Self-Evolving Personal Agents 89 

from the smudging parameters required by its proof [66]. These network, training and inference costs attach to different observers and should be reported at their respective interfaces. 

Weight-side suppression and persistent withdrawal. In JEPA-Prune, an equal-budget random and targeted pruning comparison on the same fine-tuned model separates pruning quantity from selection (v1, Section 5.1, Tables 1–2). Its evaluation measures extraction and utility immediately after the intervention. The effect of additional adaptation and recovery remains a separate question (Section 7) [110]. A personal agent may also recover the same fact from a retained store. Detecting that path requires the later-activation and recipient probes in Supplementary Section S6.3. 

S6.5 Bounded Rescreening and Sensitivity of Main Table 4 

Candidate set. On 19 November 2026 we rechecked 12 distinct records against the 10 November literature cutoff. We took three anchors per scenario: prompt, read, and host mechanisms; label, tuple, and skill-admission mechanisms; and non-use, lineage, and model-removal mechanisms. We paired each trio with one previously excluded record covering a similar operation: attributed retrieval (Agent Zero Memory, Stage 4 scope exclusions), feedback-gated skill learning (Stage 1 R07), and learned memory deletion (Stage 15 Crossref W4). We checked the cited archival or pinned primary texts at the locators below. This purposive rescreen tests the boundary decisions supporting Main Table 4. It is neither a new literature search nor a reconstruction of the historical Stage 2 exclusion trail. 

Evidence rules. C (component): Keep a proof or measurement that addresses the mapped input, operation, and observer or behavioral endpoint; distinguish a proof, a scoped outcome, and a proxy. Exclude ordinary utility, attribution, or state management without a protection result. D* (direct outcome): Require a measured outcome at the claimed interface after the affected state carries into a later interaction. A proof, fixed-store release test, candidate-only test, or workload performance result alone does not qualify. Non-use qualifies only for the reader’s behavior. To support the 

linked-view claim, evidence must cover the same fact across booking, committed skill reuse, and withdrawal for an observer retaining earlier outputs. D* is a sensitivity filter, not a recoding of 

D/S/T.

Case and record Primary locator and result checked Disposition and sensitivity 

Withdraw boundary: AgeMem ACL archival, §3.2 Table 1 and §4: learned DELETE tool; task and memory-management evaluation. Keep excluded under C and D*. An available deletion action is not a test of private residue or authorized forgetting. Under C, nine anchors remain eligible at their stated scope and the three boundary exclusions stand. Under D*, only the two withdrawal anchors provide direct evidence, for reader non-use or tracked-memory outcomes. Booking remains formal transfer plus component evidence; label and tuple results remain conditional, and skill admission remains a proxy. None of the 12 meets the linked-view criterion. The stricter rule changes which findings count as direct validation, not the mathematical scope exclusions or the original corpus counts and codes. These results apply to the checked set, not to the entire literature.
